, and the formula

0=\mathbf{D}_{\mu}\big(\kappa^{-1}\cdot\kappa\big)=\mathbf{D}_{\mu}\kappa^{-1}\kappa+\kappa^{-1}\mathbf{D}_{\mu}\kappa+\sum_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}c_{\mu\nu\nu^{\prime}}\mathbf{D}_{\nu}\kappa^{-1}\mathbf{D}_{\nu^{\prime}}\kappa.

For convenience we omit the dependence on
\xi
for the quantities we consider below. Setting
K_{n}=\sum_{\mu}\|\mathbf{D}_{\mu}\kappa^{-n}\|
, we obtain by the Leibniz property again

K_{n+1}\leq K_{n}|\xi|^{-1}+C_{1}|\xi|^{-(n+2)}+C_{2}K_{n}|\xi|^{-2}.

Inductively, this implies

K_{n}\leq C_{1}(n+1)|\xi|^{-(n+1)}\big(1+C_{2}|\xi|^{-1}\big)^{n+1},

Using the Leibniz property again, we find

\mathbf{D}_{\mu}\kappa^{-n}=\kappa^{-(n-1)}\mathbf{D}_{\mu}\kappa^{-1}+\kappa^{-1}\mathbf{D}_{\mu}\kappa^{-(n-1)}+\sum_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}c_{\mu\nu\nu^{\prime}}\mathbf{D}_{\nu}\kappa^{-(n-1)}\mathbf{D}_{\nu^{\prime}}\kappa^{-1}.

Inductively this gives (noting that by our choice,
c_{\mu\nu\nu^{\prime}}
is either 0 or 1)

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{D}_{\mu}\kappa^{-n}-\frac{n\mathbf{D}_{\mu}\kappa^{-1}}{\kappa^{n+1}}\right\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k=0}|\xi|^{k-2}K_{n-1-k}(\xi)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{n^{2}\big(1+C|\xi|^{-1}\big)^{n+1}}{|\xi|^{n+2}}.

Finally it suffices to notice that
\mathbf{D}_{\mu}\kappa^{-1}=-\kappa^{-2}\mathbf{D}_{\mu}\kappa
plus a symbol of order
-3
.
∎

We then verify that the notion of order for quasi-homogeneous symbols in Definition
4.5
coincides with Definition
2.6
. Furthermore, the difference of a quasi-homogeneous symbol can be computed by an approximate Leibniz rule.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
\kappa
be the symbol of
|\nabla|
on
\mathbb{G}
,
b
be the symbol of a vector field on
\mathbb{G}
. Let
f
be a bounded holomorphic function defined near
z=0
on the complex plane, covering the closed disk of radius
R_{0}:=\sup_{x,\xi}|\xi|^{-1}\|b(x,\xi)\|
.

(1) The quasi-homogeneous symbol
f(b/\kappa)
is of order 0 in the sense of Definition
2.6
, and the difference
\mathbf{D}_{\mu}f(b/\kappa)
is such that

\mathbf{D}_{\mu}f\left(\frac{b(x,\xi)}{\kappa(\xi)}\right)-\frac{1}{\kappa(\xi)}f^{\prime}\left(\frac{b(x,\xi)}{\kappa(\xi)}\right)\mathbf{D}_{\mu}b-\frac{\mathbf{D}_{\mu}\kappa(\xi)}{\kappa(\xi)^{2}}f^{\prime}\left(\frac{b(x,\xi)}{\kappa(\xi)}\right)b(x,\xi)

is a symbol of order
-2
.

(2) If
a
is the symbol of another vector field, then the commutator
[f(b/\kappa),a]
is still a symbol of order
0
.

(3) If
b
has at least
C^{1}
coefficients, then for any left-invariant vector field
X
, the symbol
Xf(b/\kappa)
is of order
0
, and

Xf\left(\frac{b}{\kappa}\right)-\frac{1}{\kappa}f^{\prime}\left(\frac{b}{\kappa}\right)Xb

is a symbol of order
-1
.

Corollary 4.5.1
.

Under the same assumptions of Proposition
4.6
, if in addition the vector field
\mathbf{Op}(b)
is of class
C^{r}_{*}
for
r>0
, then the symbol
\kappa^{m}f(b/\kappa)
is of class
\mathcal{A}^{m}_{r}
.

Thus the symbolic calculus formulas are easier to manipulate with for para-differential operators corresponding to quasi-homogeneous symbols.

Proof of Proposition
4.6
.

The proof is basically a repeated application of majorant power series. There is a simple fact that we will keep using: if
b
is the symbol of a vector field, then
\mathbf{D}_{\mu}b
is just a scalar function of
x
, hence commutes with any symbol. In fact, if
X
is a left-invariant vector field, then
\mathbf{D}_{\mu}\sigma[X]=(XQ_{\mu})(e)
by formula (
2.7
), which is a number. In the following, it obviously suffices to prove the estimates for representations
\xi
which are sufficiently “large” in the dominance order (i.e.
|\xi|
sufficiently large).

(1) Suppose

f(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}c_{n}z^{n},

and set
R
to be the radius of convergence of this power series. By assumption,
R_{0}=\sup_{x,\xi}|\xi|^{-1}\|b(x,\xi)\|<R
.

It suffices to prove that the expression in the statement has operator norm bounded by
|\xi|^{-2}
, because it is in fact still a power series in
b/\kappa
multiplied by
\{\mathbf{D}_{\nu}b\}_{\nu}
and
\mathbf{D}_{\mu}\kappa
. For convenience we omit the dependence on
\xi
below.

We first estimate the norm
B_{n}:=\sum_{\mu}\big\|\mathbf{D}_{\mu}(b^{n})\big\|
. By the Leibniz property we find

B_{n}\leq C_{1}(R_{0}|\xi|)^{n-1}+(R_{0}|\xi|+C_{2})B_{n-1},

so some elementary manipulation with geometric progression gives

B_{n}\lesssim n|\xi|^{-1}(R_{0}|\xi|+C_{2})^{n}.

The implicit constant does not depend on
\xi
. Similarly as in Proposition
4.5
, we may use this estimate inductively to
\mathbf{D}_{\mu}(b^{n})
to conclude

\displaystyle\big\|\mathbf{D}_{\mu}(b^{n})-nb^{n-1}\mathbf{D}_{\mu}b\big\|\lesssim n^{2}|\xi|^{-2}(R_{0}|\xi|+C_{2})^{n}

Combining this and Proppsition
4.5
, we find that the operator norm of the quantity in the statement has a majorant series

\frac{1}{|\xi|^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n^{2}|c_{n}|\frac{(R_{0}|\xi|+C_{2})^{n}+(R_{0}|\xi|+C)^{n}}{|\xi|^{n}}.

For
|\xi|
sufficiently large, Cauchy estimate for derivatives of a holomorphic function shows that the series is absolutely convergent and sums to a bounded function. This proves the claim.

(2) It suffices to notice that
[a,b]=ab-ba
is still the symbol of a vector field. Furthermore, using the Leibniz property of Lie brackets, we have

[a,b^{n}]=[a,b]b^{n-1}+b[a,b^{n-1}],

implying

\big\|[a,b^{n}]\big\|\lesssim n(R_{0}|\xi|)^{n}.

The rest of the estimate is identical to that in (1).

(3) The proof is of the same spirit of (2): in fact,

X(b^{n})=nb^{n-1}Xb+\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}b^{n-k-1}[Xb,b^{k}].

Note that
[Xb,b^{k}]
is still the symbol of a classical differential operator, whose norm at
\xi
is controlled by

\big\|[Xb,b^{k}]\big\|\lesssim_{\mathbb{G}}|b|_{C^{1}}k(R_{0}|\xi|)^{k},

as in (2). Here
|b|_{C^{1}}
stands for the
C^{1}
norm of the coefficients. Thus

\big\|X(b^{n})-nb^{n-1}Xb\big\|\lesssim n^{2}(R_{0}|\xi|)^{n-1}|b|_{C^{1}}

and the majorant series argument is sufficient to imply the desired result.
∎

We conclude this subsection with a proposition regarding symbols that involve very rough coefficients.

Proposition 4.7
.

Suppose
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}c_{n}z^{n}
is a convergent power series near
z=0
. Fix
r>0
. Let
s>n/2
,
s>r
. Let
a,b
be symbols of vector fields on
\mathbb{G}
, with
a
of merely
C^{-r}_{*}
regularity in
x
and
b
of
H^{s}
regularity in
x
, with

\sup_{\xi\in\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\frac{\|b(x,\xi)\|_{H^{s}_{x}}}{|\xi|}

suitably small. Define

[b]_{n}a:=ab^{n}+bab^{n-1}+\cdots+b^{n}a.

Then

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}c_{n}\kappa^{-n}[b]_{n}a

is a symbol of class
\mathcal{A}^{0}_{-r}(\mathbb{G})
.

Proof.

Suppose
c
is any
H^{s}
vector field on
\mathbb{G}
. Let us recall the para-product decomposition Theorem
3.10
to analyze the product
ac
. With a little abuse of notation, we write
ac
to represent any coefficient appearing in the product symbol
ac
. Then

ac=T_{a}c+T_{c}a+R(a,c).

By Proposition
4.4
,
T_{a}
is a para-differential operator of order
r
, so

|T_{a}c|_{C^{-r}_{*}}\leq C\|T_{a}c\|_{H^{s-r}}\leq C|a|_{C^{-r}_{*}}\|c\|_{H^{s}}.

Since
T_{c}
is a para-differential operator of order 0,
|T_{c}a|_{C^{-r}_{*}}\leq C|a|_{C^{-r}_{*}}\|c\|_{H^{s}}
. Finally, from the proof of Theorem
3.10
, the symbol
R[a]\in\mathscr{S}^{r}_{1,1}
, so by Stein’s theorem,

|R(a,c)|_{C^{-r}_{*}}\leq C\|R(a,c)\|_{H^{s-r}}\leq C|a|_{C^{-r}_{*}}\|c\|_{H^{s}}.

To summarize,
|ac|_{C^{-r}_{*}}\leq C|a|_{C^{-r}_{*}}\|c\|_{H^{s}}
.

We now use this to analyze the product
b^{k}ab^{n-k}
. Since
H^{s}
is a Banach algebra, we have

|ab^{n-k}|_{C^{-r}_{*}}\leq C|a|_{C^{-r}_{*}}\|b^{n-k}\|_{H^{s}}\leq C_{s}^{n-k}|a|_{C^{-r}_{*}}\|b\|_{H^{s}}^{n-k},

which further implies

|b^{k}ab^{n-k}|_{C^{-r}_{*}}\leq C_{s}^{n}|a|_{C^{-r}_{*}}\|b\|_{H^{s}}^{n}.

Thus if
\sup_{\xi\in\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}|\xi|^{-1}\|b(x,\xi)\|_{H^{s}_{x}}
is sufficiently small, then the series of matrices

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}c_{n}\kappa^{-n}[b]_{n}a

will have an absolutely convergent majorant series with respect to the
C^{-r}_{*}
norm. This concludes the proof.
∎

5.
Global Symbolic Calculus on
\mathrm{SU}(2)
and
\mathbb{S}^{2}

We apply the general theory developed so far to construct para-differetial calculus on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
, the standard 2-sphere. Although
\mathbb{S}^{2}
cannot be endowed with a Lie group structure, it still inherits a symbolic calculus as
\mathrm{SU}(2)
-homogeneous space in a simple manner. The para-differential calculus on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
will be constructed in this way. We start by formulating a global symbolic calculus on
\mathrm{SU}(2)
, and then pass to
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.

5.1.
Representation Theory of
\mathrm{SU}(2)

Let us first sketch the representation theory of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
. An element of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
will be denoted as

x=\left(\begin{matrix}x_{1}&x_{2}\\
-\bar{x}_{2}&\bar{x}_{1}\end{matrix}\right),\quad|x_{1}|^{2}+|x_{2}|^{2}=1.

The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{su}(2)
, being the set of all skew-Hermitian matrices with trace 0, can be identified with the tangent space at
\mathrm{Id}\in\mathrm{SU}(2)
and also the Lie algebra of left-invariant vector fields. It is spanned by the real-linear combination of (scaled) Pauli matrices

\frac{1}{2}\left(\begin{matrix}0&i\\
i&0\end{matrix}\right),\quad\frac{1}{2}\left(\begin{matrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{matrix}\right),\quad\frac{1}{2}\left(\begin{matrix}i&0\\
0&-i\end{matrix}\right).

They correspond to the 1-dimensional subgroups

\omega_{1}(t)=\left(\begin{matrix}\cos\frac{t}{2}&i\sin\frac{t}{2}\\
i\sin\frac{t}{2}&\cos\frac{t}{2}\end{matrix}\right),\quad\omega_{2}(t)=\left(\begin{matrix}\cos\frac{t}{2}&-\sin\frac{t}{2}\\
\sin\frac{t}{2}&\cos\frac{t}{2}\end{matrix}\right),\quad\omega_{3}(t)=\left(\begin{matrix}e^{it/2}&0\\
0&e^{-it/2}\end{matrix}\right)

respectively. We denote these subgroups as
\mathbf{T}_{1,2,3}
.

We employ the Euler angles as parameterization of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
. For

\varphi\in[0,2\pi),\quad\theta\in[0,\pi],\quad\psi\in[-2\pi,2\pi),

an element of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
outside a lower dimensional closed subset is factorized as

(5.1)

\left(\begin{matrix}e^{i(\varphi+\psi)/2}\cos\frac{\theta}{2}&ie^{i(\varphi-\psi)/2}\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\\
ie^{-i(\varphi-\psi)/2}\sin\frac{\theta}{2}&e^{-i(\varphi+\psi)/2}\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\end{matrix}\right)=\left(\begin{matrix}e^{i\varphi/2}&0\\
0&e^{-i\varphi/2}\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}\cos\frac{\theta}{2}&i\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\\
i\sin\frac{\theta}{2}&\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}e^{i\psi/2}&0\\
0&e^{-i\psi/2}\end{matrix}\right).

The parameters
\varphi\in[0,2\pi),\,\theta\in(0,\pi),\,\psi\in[-2\pi,2\pi)
are in 1-1 correspondence with elements of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
excluding a lower dimensional closed subset (which corresponds to
\theta=\pm\pi
). We shall denote the element with Euler angles
\varphi,\theta,\psi
as
\Omega(\varphi,\theta,\psi)
. The factorization (
5.1
) can be re-written as

(5.2)

\Omega(\varphi,\theta,\psi)=\omega_{3}(\varphi)\omega_{1}(\theta)\omega_{3}(\psi)\in\mathbf{T}_{3}\cdot\mathbf{T}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{T}_{3}.

The Killing form of
\mathfrak{su}(2)
is
4\Tr(XY)
for
X,Y\in\mathfrak{su}(2)
. If we set the negative of this Killing form as the inner product on
\mathfrak{su}(2)
, then the matrices
\omega_{1,2,3}^{\prime}(0)
form an orthonormal basis. Thus, the Riemann metric on
\mathrm{SU}(2)
under the Euler angles is

G_{0}=d\theta^{2}+d\varphi^{2}+2\cos\theta d\varphi d\psi+d\psi^{2},

or in matrix form

\left(\begin{matrix}(G_{0})_{\theta\theta}&(G_{0})_{\theta\varphi}&(G_{0})_{\theta\psi}\\
*&(G_{0})_{\varphi\varphi}&(G_{0})_{\varphi\psi}\\
*&*&(G_{0})_{\psi\psi}\end{matrix}\right)=\left(\begin{matrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&\cos\theta\\
0&\cos\theta&1\end{matrix}\right).

If we identify
\mathrm{SU}(2)
with the unit 3-sphere in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
, then
G_{0}
is 4 times the inherited metric on unit 3-sphere. The Laplacian of
G_{0}
is

\Delta_{G_{0}}=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\theta^{2}}+\frac{\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}+\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\theta}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\varphi^{2}}-\frac{2\cos\theta}{\sin^{2}\theta}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\varphi\partial\psi}+\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\theta}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\psi^{2}}.

In Chapter 3 of
[
Vil78
]
, Vilenkin explicitly computed the left-invariant vector fields on
\mathrm{SU}(2)
corresponding to the subgroups
\omega_{1,2,3}(t)
. We denote them by
X_{1,2,3}
respectively. The action of
X_{j}
is

(X_{j}f)(x):=\frac{d}{dt}f(x\omega_{j}(t))\Big|_{t=0}.

They are given by, respectively,

(5.3)

\displaystyle X_{1}

\displaystyle=\cos\psi\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}+\frac{\sin\psi}{\sin\theta}\frac{\partial}{\partial\varphi}-\frac{\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}\sin\psi\frac{\partial}{\partial\psi},

\displaystyle X_{2}

\displaystyle=-\sin\psi\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}+\frac{\cos\psi}{\sin\theta}\frac{\partial}{\partial\varphi}-\frac{\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}\cos\psi\frac{\partial}{\partial\psi},

\displaystyle X_{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial\psi}.

It is directly verified that these vector fields form an orthonormal basis in the tangent space of any element in
\mathrm{SU}(2)
, and
\Delta_{G_{0}}=X_{1}^{2}+X_{2}^{2}+X_{3}^{2}
. However, for symbolic calculus, it is more convenient to consider certain linear combination of the
X_{j}
’s since it is more convenient for the symbols to contain more 0’s. We thus introduce the
creation, annihilation and neutral
operators

(5.4)

\displaystyle\partial_{[}+]

\displaystyle=iX_{1}-X_{2}=e^{-i\psi}\left(i\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}-\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\frac{\partial}{\partial\varphi}+\frac{\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}\frac{\partial}{\partial\psi}\right),

\displaystyle\partial_{[}-]

\displaystyle=iX_{1}+X_{2}=e^{i\psi}\left(i\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}+\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\frac{\partial}{\partial\varphi}-\frac{\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}\frac{\partial}{\partial\psi}\right),

\displaystyle\partial_{[}0]

\displaystyle=iX_{3}=i\frac{\partial}{\partial\psi},

just as in
[
Vil78
]
or
[
RT13
]
. Thus

\Delta_{G_{0}}=-\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{[}-]\partial_{[}+]+\partial_{[}+]\partial_{[}-])-\partial_{[}0]^{2},

and the commutator relations for
\mathfrak{su}(2)
hold:

[\partial_{[}0],\partial_{[}+]]=\partial_{[}+],\quad[\partial_{[}-],\partial_{[}0]]=\partial_{[}-],\quad[\partial_{[}+],\partial_{[}-]]=2\partial_{[}0].

The table of irreducible unitary representations of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
can be found in any standard textbook on representation theory. We sketch the result as follows. Since the group
\mathrm{SU}(2)
has rank 1, one can label irreducible unitary representations of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
by a parameter
l\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the set of half integers. The irreducible unitary representation with label
l
has dimension
2l+1
. The
l
’th representation is realized as follows: setting
\mathcal{H}_{[}l]
to be the space of complex homogeneous polynomials in a two-dimensional variable
z\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
with degree
2l
, the action of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
on
\mathcal{H}_{[}l]
is realized as

\big(T^{l}(x)f\big)(z):=f(xz).

The Hermite structure on
\mathcal{H}_{[}l]
is defined by fixing an orthonormal basis

\frac{z_{1}^{l-k}z_{2}^{l+k}}{\sqrt{(l-k)!(l+k)!}},\quad k=-l,\,-l+1,\,\cdots,\,l-1,\,l.

Following
[
Vil78
]
, we use
T^{l}=(T^{l}_{nm})
,
m,n=-l,\,-l+1,\,\cdots,\,l-1,\,l
, to denote the matrix elements of the representation labeled by
l
under the basis fixed above. We fix the convention that
n
labels the rows of
T^{l}
and
m
labels the columns of
T^{l}
. In Euler angles,

T^{l}_{nm}\big(\Omega(\varphi,\theta,\psi)\big)=e^{-i(n\varphi+m\psi)}P^{l}_{nm}(\cos\theta),

where

P^{l}_{nm}(z)=\frac{2^{-l}(-1)^{l-m}i^{m-n}}{\sqrt{(l-m)!(l+m)!}}\sqrt{\frac{(l+n)!}{(l-n)!}}\cdot\frac{(1-z)^{(m-n)/2}}{(1+z)^{(m+n)/2}}\left(\frac{d}{dz}\right)^{l-n}\left((1-z)^{l-m}(1+z)^{l+m}\right).

For example,
l=0
gives the trivial representation
\mathrm{SU}(2)\to\{1\}
; for
l=1/2
, we obtain the fundamental representation, which is also a faithful:
T^{1/2}(\Omega(\varphi,\theta,\psi))=\Omega(\varphi,\theta,\psi)
, and the entries are

\bordermatrix{&m=-\frac{1}{2}&m=\frac{1}{2}\cr n=-\frac{1}{2}&e^{i(\varphi+\psi)/2}\cos\frac{\theta}{2}&ie^{i(\varphi-\psi)/2}\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\cr n=\frac{1}{2}&ie^{-i(\varphi-\psi)/2}\sin\frac{\theta}{2}&e^{-i(\varphi+\psi)/2}\cos\frac{\theta}{2}};

for
l=1
, the entries of
T^{1}(\Omega(\varphi,\theta,\psi))
are

\bordermatrix{&m=-1&m=0&m=1\cr n=-1&e^{i(\varphi+\psi)/2}\cos^{2}\frac{\theta}{2}&ie^{i\varphi}\frac{\sin\theta}{\sqrt{2}}&-e^{i(\varphi-\psi)/2}\sin^{2}\frac{\theta}{2}\cr n=0&ie^{i\psi}\frac{\sin\theta}{\sqrt{2}}&\cos\theta&ie^{-i\psi}\frac{\sin\theta}{\sqrt{2}}\cr n=1&-e^{-i(\varphi-\psi)/2}\sin^{2}\frac{\theta}{2}&ie^{-i\varphi}\frac{\sin\theta}{\sqrt{2}}&e^{-i(\varphi+\psi)/2}\cos^{2}\frac{\theta}{2}};

From now on, we shall write
\widehat{f}(l)
for the Fourier transform of
f\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathrm{SU}(2))
:

(5.5)

\displaystyle\widehat{f}(l)_{mn}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathrm{SU}(2)}f(x)\overline{T^{l}_{nm}}(x)dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{4\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}f\big(\Omega(\varphi,\theta,\psi)\big)\overline{T^{l}_{nm}}\big(\Omega(\varphi,\theta,\psi)\big)\sin\theta\cdot d\varphi d\theta d\psi.

The Peter-Weyl theorem
2.1
now takes the form

f(x)=\sum_{l\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}_{0}}(2l+1)\sum_{m,n=-l}^{l}\widehat{f}(l)_{mn}T^{l}_{nm}(x).

Here the convention of summation for
m,n
is that they exhaust all numbers
-l,\,-l+1,\,\cdots,\,l-1,\,l
. The functions
\{T^{l}_{nm}\}_{m,n=-l}^{l}
span the eigenspace
\mathcal{H}_{[}l]
of
\Delta_{G_{0}}
with eigenvalue
-l(l+1)
.

Since
\mathrm{SU}(2)
can be identified as the unit 3-sphere in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
, the functions
\{T^{l}_{nm}\}_{m,n=-l}^{l}
are exactly the spherical harmonics on 3-sphere of degree
2l+1
. The spectral localization property, i.e. Corollary
2.3.1
, now becomes the well-known fact that the product of spherical harmonics of degree
p
and
q
is a linear combination of spherical harmonics of degree between
|p-q|
and
p+q
. However, it should be emphasized that the Euler angle parameterization
does not
coincide with the spherical coordinate parameterization of spherical harmonics.

5.2.
Symbolic Calculus on
\mathrm{SU}(2)

We now construct pseudo-differential calculus on
\mathrm{SU}(2)
. For

x=\left(\begin{matrix}x_{1}&x_{2}\\
-\bar{x}_{2}&\bar{x}_{1}\end{matrix}\right)\in\mathrm{SU}(2),

the functions

x_{1}-1,\quad\bar{x}_{1}-1,\quad x_{2},\quad-\bar{x}_{2}

form a strongly RT-admissible tuple of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
defined in
2.2
, which is also the fundamental tuple of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
defined in (
2.9
). The only disadvantage is that this tuple consists of 4 elements and can bring unnecessary tedium for calculation. Following
[
RT13
]
, we introduce instead

(5.6)

\mathscr{Q}_{+}(x)=-\bar{x}_{2},\quad\mathscr{Q}_{-}(x)=x_{2},\quad\mathscr{Q}_{0}(x)=x_{1}-\bar{x}_{1}.

The tuple
\mathscr{Q}=(\mathscr{Q}_{+},\mathscr{Q}_{-},\mathscr{Q}_{0})
is RT-admissible but not strongly RT-admissible, since
-\mathrm{Id}
is the other common zero of them besides
\mathrm{Id}
. A direct computation gives

(\partial_{[}+]\mathscr{Q}_{+})(\mathrm{Id})=(\partial_{[}-]\mathscr{Q}_{-})(\mathrm{Id})=(\partial_{[}0]\mathscr{Q}_{0})(\mathrm{Id})=-1.

Taylor’s formula, i.e. Proposition
2.2
is then valid for
q
and the differential operators
\partial_{[}+],\partial_{[}-],\partial_{[}0]
. Note that the higher order left-invariant differential operators in Taylor’s formula, denoted by
\partial_{x}^{(\alpha)}
from now on, have to be defined inductively, since
\partial_{[}+],\partial_{[}-],\partial_{[}0]
do not commute.

Since the dual of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
is labelled by
l\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we write
a(x,l)\in\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{H}_{[}l])
for a symbol
a
on
\mathrm{SU}(2)
. The symbolic calculus on
\mathrm{SU}(2)
then starts with the symbol of composition

(5.7)

(a\#_{r;\mathscr{Q}}b)(x,l)=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq r}\mathbf{D}^{\alpha}_{\mathscr{Q},l}a(x,l)\cdot\partial_{x}^{(\alpha)}b(x,l),

and the symbol of adjoint

(5.8)

a^{\bullet;r,\mathscr{Q}}(x,l)=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq r}\mathbf{D}^{\alpha}_{\mathscr{Q},l}\partial_{x}^{(\alpha)}a^{*}(x,l).

We directly cite the following results from
[
RT09
]
:

Theorem 5.1
.

The symbols
\sigma_{+}
,
\sigma_{-}
and
\sigma_{0}
of
\partial_{[}+]
,
\partial_{[}-]
and
\partial_{[}0]
respectively are all Fourier multipliers:

\displaystyle\sigma_{+}(l)_{mn}

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{(l-n)(l+n+1)}\delta_{m,n+1}

\displaystyle\sigma_{-}(l)_{mn}

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{(l+n)(l-n+1)}\delta_{m,n-1}

\displaystyle\sigma_{0}(l)_{mn}

\displaystyle=n\delta_{mn}.

Theorem 5.2
.

Suppose
a
is a Fourier multiplier on
\mathrm{SU}(2)
, i.e. for each
l\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the value
a(l)\in\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{H}_{[}l])
. Then the RT difference operators
\mathbf{D}_{+}
,
\mathbf{D}_{-}
,
\mathbf{D}_{0}
corresponding to
\mathscr{Q}_{+}
,
\mathscr{Q}_{-}
,
\mathscr{Q}_{0}
respectively are

(\mathbf{D}_{+}a)(l)_{nm}=\frac{\sqrt{(l+m)(l-n)}}{2l+1}a(l^{-})_{n^{+}m^{-}}-\frac{\sqrt{(l-m+1)(l+n+1)}}{2l+1}a(l^{+})_{n^{+}m^{-}},

(\mathbf{D}_{-}a)(l)_{nm}=\frac{\sqrt{(l-m)(l+n)}}{2l+1}a(l^{-})_{n^{-}m^{+}}-\frac{\sqrt{(l+m+1)(l-n+1)}}{2l+1}a(l^{+})_{n^{-}m^{+}},

\displaystyle(\mathbf{D}_{0}a)(l)_{nm}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{(l-m)(l-n)}}{2l+1}a(l^{-})_{n^{+}m^{+}}+\frac{\sqrt{(l+m+1)(l+n+1)}}{2l+1}a(l^{+})_{n^{+}m^{+}}

\displaystyle-\frac{\sqrt{(l+m)(l+n)}}{2l+1}a(l^{-})_{n^{-}m^{-}}-\frac{\sqrt{(l-m+1)(l-n+1)}}{2l+1}a(l^{+})_{n^{-}m^{-}}

Here
k^{\pm}=k\pm 1/2
. In particular,

\mathbf{D}_{\mu}\sigma_{\nu}=\delta_{\mu\nu}\cdot\sigma_{\mathrm{Id}},\quad\mu,\nu=+,\,-,\,0.

Remark 5.1
.

We are actually able to manipulate para-differential operators arising from classical differential operators on
\mathrm{SU}(2)
fairly easily. For example, let
Y=a\partial_{[}+]
and
Z=b\partial_{[}-]
be vector fields with coefficients
a,b\in C^{r}
with
r>1
. Then the commutator

[Y,Z]=a\partial_{[}+]b\partial_{[}-]-b\partial_{[}-]a\partial_{[}+]+2ab\partial_{[}0]

On the other hand, the symbols of
Y,Z
are respectively

\sigma[Y]=a\sigma_{+},\quad\sigma[Z]=b\sigma_{-},

so a direct computation using Theorem
5.2
implies a precise equality

\displaystyle\sigma[Y]\#_{r;\mathscr{Q}}\sigma[Z]-\sigma[Z]\#_{r;\mathscr{Q}}\sigma[Y]

\displaystyle=ab[\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-}]+a\mathbf{D}_{+}\sigma_{+}\partial_{[}+]b\sigma_{-}-b\mathbf{D}_{-}\sigma_{-}\partial_{[}-]a\sigma_{+}

\displaystyle=2ab\sigma_{0}+a\partial_{[}+]b\sigma_{-}-b\partial_{[}-]a\sigma_{+}.

Consequently, Theorem
4.2
ensures that
[T_{Y},T_{Z}]
is exactly the para-differential operator corresponding to
[Y,Z]
, modulo a smoothing operator in
\mathbf{Op}\Sigma_{<1/2}^{1-r}
. Consequently, with
\{Y,Z\}
being the symbol of
[Y,Z]
,

[T_{Y},T_{Z}]=T_{\{Y,Z\}}+\mathbf{Op}\Sigma_{<1/2}^{1-r}.

5.3.
Passage to the 2-Sphere via Hopf Fiberation

We finally describe how pseudo-differential calculus can be constructed on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
. Although
\mathbb{S}^{2}
does not carry any Lie group structure, it is still a homogeneous space corresponding to
\mathrm{SU}(2)
, and the projection
\mathrm{SU}(2)\to\mathbb{S}^{2}
is
Hopf fiberation
, well-known in geometric topology. To be consistent with
left-invariant differential operators
we have been employing so far, following
[
RT13
]
, we consider
\mathbb{S}^{2}
as a
right

\mathrm{SU}(2)
-homogeneous space, that is, the set of
right cosets

\mathbf{T}_{3}\setminus\mathrm{SU}(2):=\Big\{\mathbf{T}_{3}\cdot x:\,x\in\mathrm{SU}(2)\Big\}

endowed with the quotient topology, on which
\mathrm{SU}(2)

acts from the right
. A point that one should keep in notice is that, in the Euler angle representation as in (
5.2
), the angle
\varphi\in[0,2\pi)
only exhausts half of the subgroup
\mathbf{T}_{3}
, so the orbit of a given element
\Omega(\varphi_{0},\theta_{0},\psi_{0})\in\mathrm{SU}(2)
is

\Big\{\pm\Omega(\varphi,\theta_{0},\psi_{0}):\,\varphi\in[0,2\pi)\Big\}.

We thus realize the Hopf map
\mathrm{SU}(2)\to\mathbb{S}^{2}
as

\left(\begin{matrix}x_{1}&x_{2}\\
-\bar{x}_{2}&\bar{x}_{1}\end{matrix}\right)\to\left(\begin{matrix}-2\mathrm{Im}(x_{1}\bar{x}_{2})\\
2\mathrm{Re}(x_{1}\bar{x}_{2})\\
|x_{1}|^{2}-|x_{2}|^{2}\end{matrix}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{3},\quad\text{where}\quad|x_{1}|^{2}+|x_{2}|^{2}=1,

or in Euler angles,

\Omega(\varphi,\theta,\psi)=\left(\begin{matrix}e^{i(\varphi+\psi)/2}\cos\frac{\theta}{2}&ie^{i(\varphi-\psi)/2}\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\\
ie^{-i(\varphi-\psi)/2}\sin\frac{\theta}{2}&e^{-i(\varphi+\psi)/2}\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\end{matrix}\right)\to\left(\begin{matrix}\sin\theta\cos\psi\\
\sin\theta\sin\psi\\
\cos\theta\end{matrix}\right).

We note that the Hopf fiberation is a Riemannian submersion if
\mathrm{SU}(2)
is endowed with the metric
G_{0}
and
\mathbb{S}^{2}
is endowed with the standard metric
g_{0}
.

A function on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
is lifted to
\mathrm{SU}(2)
as a function independent to the Euler angle
\varphi
and symmetric with respect to the reflection map
x\to-x
. Given a function
f
(distribution) on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
, we denote by

(5.9)

f^{\sharp}\big(\Omega(\varphi,\theta,\psi)\big):=f\big(\sin\theta\cos\psi,\sin\theta\sin\psi,\cos\theta\big)

its lift to the
\mathbf{T}_{3}
-invariant function (distribution) on
\mathrm{SU}(2)
. We refer smooth functions (distributions respectively) on
\mathrm{SU}(2)
with such symmetry as being
\mathbf{T}_{3}
-invariant
, and denote the space of them by
C^{\infty}(\mathbf{T}_{3}\setminus\mathrm{SU}(2))
(
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{T}_{3}\setminus\mathrm{SU}(2))
respectively). We emphasize that sometimes it is necessary to distinguish between
C^{\infty}(\mathbf{T}_{3}\setminus\mathrm{SU}(2))
and
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
. Further definitions can be formulated for operators and symbols:

Definition 5.1
.

A linear operator
A:C^{\infty}(\mathrm{SU}(2))\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathrm{SU}(2))
is said to be
\mathbf{T}_{3}
-invariant, if it maps
C^{\infty}(\mathbf{T}_{3}\setminus\mathrm{SU}(2))
to
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbf{T}_{3}\setminus\mathrm{SU}(2))
.

Definition 5.2
.

A symbol
a(x,l)
on
\mathrm{SU}(2)
is said to be
\mathbf{T}_{3}
-invariant, if for any element
\omega_{3}(t)\in\mathbf{T}_{3}
, there holds
a(\omega_{3}(t)x,l)=a(x,l)
, for all
x\in\mathrm{SU}(2)
and
l\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}_{0}
. In Euler angles,
\mathbf{T}_{3}
-invariant symbols are exactly those that do not depend on the Euler angle
\varphi
and remain invariant under reflection
x\to-x
.

Obviously an operator is
\mathbf{T}_{3}
-invariant if and only if its symbol is
\mathbf{T}_{3}
-invariant. We can thus lift pseudo-differential operators on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
to
\mathrm{SU}(2)
via the realization of Hopf fiberation that we fixed above. There are necessarily infinitely many ways of lifting and there is no “natural” one among them. However, for classical differential operators and pseudo-differential operators constructed out of the Laplacian
\Delta_{g_{0}}
, lifting to
\mathrm{SU}(2)
as
\Delta_{G_{0}}
is direct. The advantage of this approach is that operators on
\mathrm{SU}(2)
admit an explicitly manipulable symbolic calculus. In the formulas for symbolic calculus, if
a,b
are both
\mathbf{T}_{3}
-invariant, then the symbols

(5.10)

(a\#_{r;\mathscr{Q}}b)(x,l)=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq r}\mathbf{D}^{\alpha}_{\mathscr{Q},l}a(x,l)\cdot\partial_{x}^{(\alpha)}b(x,l),

and

(5.11)

a^{\bullet;r,\mathscr{Q}}(x,l)=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq r}\mathbf{D}^{\alpha}_{\mathscr{Q},l}\partial_{x}^{(\alpha)}a^{*}(x,l).

are still
\mathbf{T}_{3}
-invariant, since the differential operators
\partial_{x}^{(\alpha)}
are left-invariant, hence specifically invariant under left translation via
\omega_{3}(\varphi)
. Briefly speaking,
symbolic calculus for
\mathbf{T}_{3}
-invariant operators preserves
\mathbf{T}_{3}
-invariance
. Consequently, symbolic calculus on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
can be constructed by lifting operators on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
to
\mathbf{T}_{3}
-invariant operators on
\mathrm{SU}(2)
, and manipulated without concern for
\mathbf{T}_{3}
-invariance. For a
\mathbf{T}_{3}
-invariant rough symbol
a\in\mathcal{A}^{m}_{r}
, the corresponding para-differential operator
T_{a}:=\mathbf{Op}(a^{\chi})
is still
\mathbf{T}_{3}
-invariant, since spectral cut-off preserves
\mathbf{T}_{3}
-invariance.

It is more illustrative to write down the Fourier series development. For example, given a function
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
, we denote its lift to
\mathrm{SU}(2)
as
f^{\sharp}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbf{T}_{3}\setminus\mathrm{SU}(2))
. By (
5.5
), The Fourier transform
\widehat{f^{\sharp}}(l)
does not vanish only when
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, and for
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the only possible non-vanishing entries are those with
m=0
, i.e. the column with index 0. In that case, we have

\widehat{f^{\sharp}}(l)_{0n}=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}f(x)Y^{-n}_{l}(x)d\mu_{0},

with
Y^{n}_{l}(x)
being the standard complex spherical harmonic on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
. Furthermore, for a
\mathbf{T}_{3}
-invariant symbol
a(x,l)
, the action
\mathbf{Op}(a)f^{\sharp}
reads

\sum_{l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}(2l+1)\Tr\big[a(x,l)\widehat{f^{\sharp}}(l)T^{l}(x)\big].

Under Euler angle representation, the matrix
\widehat{f^{\sharp}}(l)T^{l}(x)
consists of entries depending only on
\psi
and
\theta
, so each summand, hence
\mathbf{Op}(a)f^{\sharp}
, is a function of Euler angles
\theta
and
\psi
only. The reflection invariance is checked similarly. As a result,
\mathbf{Op}(a)f^{\sharp}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbf{T}_{3}\setminus\mathrm{SU}(2))
, hence projects to a function defined on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.

6.
Para-linearization of Dirichlet-Neumann Operator for a Distorted Sphere

In this section, we prove the para-linearization formula for the Dirichlet-Neumann operator of a distorted sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We are going to lift our discussion to
\mathrm{SU}(2)
, so that the toolbox of para-differential calculus is available. The idea is to “factorize” the Laplacian and then reduce to the boundary.

6.1.
The Dirichlet-Neumann Operator

Recall that we set
\iota_{0}:\mathbb{S}^{2}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
to be the standard embedding. We write
\rho
for the Euclidean distance function to
0
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and define

\mathcal{U}=\left\{\frac{1}{2}<\rho<\frac{3}{2}\right\},

so that
\mathcal{U}
is a tubular neighbourhood of the standard unit sphere, with smooth boundary. Obviously
{\mathcal{U}}
is diffeomorphic to the product manifold
\mathbb{S}^{2}\times(-1/2,1/2)
, with smooth boundary
\mathbb{S}^{2}\times\{-1/2,1/2\}
.

We shall use
x
to mark points on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
or
\mathrm{SU}(2)
(there is usually no risk of confusion). We already used
g_{0}
to denote the induced metric and
N_{0}
to denote the outward pointing normal vector field of
\mathbb{S}^{2}
, also known as the Gauss map. Note that
N_{0}
coincides with
\iota_{0}
.

Just as shown in Figure
1
, the distorted sphere
M_{\zeta}
we shall consider will be given by the graph of a “height function”
\zeta\in C^{r}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
with
|\zeta|_{L^{\infty}}<1/2
, i.e.

M_{\zeta}:=\{(1+\zeta(x))N_{0}(x):x\in\mathbb{S}^{2}\}.

We may thus abbreviate
\iota_{\zeta}=(1+\zeta)N_{0}
, and
M_{0}=\iota_{0}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
. If
r\geq 1
,
M_{\zeta}
is
C^{r}
-diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{S}^{2}
and is itself a
C^{r}
differentiable hypersurface, but this is the highest regularity to expect for generic
\zeta\in C^{r}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
. From now on, we shall fix
r>2
.

The most convenient coordinate system that we shall use is the distorted normal coordinate with respect to
M_{0}
, that is a
C^{r}
diffeomorphism mapping a point
(x,y)\in\mathbb{S}^{2}\times[-1/2,1/2]
to

(1+\zeta(x)+y)N_{0}(x)\in\bar{\mathcal{U}}.

Thus, under our choice of coordinate, we have

\rho(x,y)=1+\zeta(x)+y.

We then pull everything on
\bar{\mathcal{U}}
back to
\mathbb{S}^{2}\times[-1/2,1/2]
through this specific diffeomorphism. As a starting point, the pulled-back Euclidean metric reads

(6.1)

g_{\mathrm{E}}=\rho^{2}g_{0}(x)+(d\zeta+dy)\otimes(d\zeta+dy),\quad\rho=1+\zeta+y.

In deriving the expression for
g_{\mathrm{E}}
, we used the second fundamental form
\langle d\iota_{0}(x),dN_{0}(x)\rangle
, where the bracket denotes the pairing
\langle a_{i}dx^{i},c_{j}dx^{j}\rangle:=(a_{i}\cdot c_{j})dx^{i}\otimes dx^{j}
between
\mathbb{R}^{3}
-valued differential 1-forms on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
. Note that for the sphere, the second fundamental form coincides with
g_{0}
. The following
C^{r}
-Riemannian manifold (with boundary) is thus isometric to
\bar{\mathcal{U}}
:

\bar{\mathcal{W}}:=\Big(\mathbb{S}^{2}\times\left[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right],g_{\mathrm{E}}\Big).

The induced metric on
M_{\zeta}
, being the hypersurface
y=0
in
\bar{\mathcal{W}}
, also takes a simple form

g_{\zeta}=(1+\zeta)^{2}g_{0}+d\zeta\otimes d\zeta.

We use the following formulas for block matrices to compute the dual metric of
g_{\mathrm{E}}
:

\left(\begin{matrix}A+cc^{\top}&c\\
c^{\top}&1\end{matrix}\right)^{-1}=\left(\begin{matrix}A^{-1}&-A^{-1}c\\
-(A^{-1}c)^{\top}&1+(A^{-1}c)\cdot c\end{matrix}\right),\quad\det\left(\begin{matrix}A+cc^{\top}&c\\
c^{\top}&1\end{matrix}\right)=\det A

for an
2\times 2
positive definite symmetric matrix
A
and
c\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We find the dual metric of
g_{\mathrm{E}}
reads

(6.2)

\displaystyle g_{\mathrm{E}}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{g_{0}^{-1}(x)}{\rho^{2}}+\left(1+\frac{|\nabla_{0}\zeta|_{g_{0}}^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\right)\partial_{y}\otimes\partial_{y}-\frac{\nabla_{0}\zeta\otimes\partial_{y}+\partial_{y}\otimes\nabla_{0}\zeta}{\rho^{2}}.

Here
g_{0}^{-1}
stands for the dual metric of
g_{0}
.

For a function
\Phi
defined on
\bar{\mathcal{U}}
, we pull it back to
\bar{\mathcal{W}}
as

(6.3)

\Psi(x,y):=\Phi\big((1+\zeta(x)+y)N_{0}(x)\big).

Regarding
\Psi
as a function defined on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
depending on parameter
y\in[-1/2,0]
, the gradient of
\Psi
is computed as

\nabla_{\mathrm{E}}\Psi=\frac{\nabla_{0}\Psi-\partial_{y}\Psi\nabla_{0}\zeta}{\rho^{2}}+\left[\left(1+\frac{|\nabla_{0}\zeta|_{g_{0}}^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\right)\partial_{y}\Psi-\frac{\nabla_{0}\zeta\cdot\nabla_{0}\Psi}{\rho^{2}}\right]\partial_{y}.

The hypersurface
y=0
corresponds to
M_{\zeta}
in
\mathcal{U}
, so the vector field

\nabla_{\mathrm{E}}y=-\frac{\nabla_{0}\zeta}{\rho^{2}}+\left(1+\frac{|\nabla_{0}\zeta|_{g_{0}}^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\right)\partial_{y}\Bigg|_{y=0}

along
\{y=0\}
is perpendicular to the hypersurface. Similarly, the hypersurface
y+\zeta(x)=0
corresponds to the undisturbed sphere in
\mathcal{U}
, so

N_{0}(x)=\partial_{y}|_{y=-\zeta(x)}.

Consequently, using the expression (
6.1
) of
g_{\mathrm{E}}
, the Dirichlet-Neumann operator
{D[\zeta]\phi}
in (
EQ
) is computed as

(6.4)

\displaystyle D[\zeta]\phi

\displaystyle=\frac{\nabla_{\mathrm{E}}\Psi\cdot\nabla_{\mathrm{E}}y}{N_{0}\cdot\nabla_{\mathrm{E}}y}\Bigg|_{y=0}

\displaystyle=\left(1+\frac{|\nabla_{0}\zeta|_{g_{0}}^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\right)\partial_{y}\Psi\Bigg|_{y=0}-\frac{\nabla_{0}\zeta\cdot\nabla_{0}\Psi}{\rho^{2}}\Bigg|_{y=0}.

The
g_{\mathrm{E}}
-Laplacian of
\Psi
, which is just the Euclidean Laplacian of
\Psi
pulled back to
\bar{\mathcal{W}}
, is computed as

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathrm{E}}\Psi

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta_{0}\Psi}{\rho^{2}}+\left(1+\frac{|\nabla_{0}\zeta|_{g_{0}}^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\right)\partial_{y}^{2}\Psi-\frac{2\nabla_{0}\zeta\cdot(\nabla_{0}\partial_{y}\Psi)}{\rho^{2}}-\frac{\Delta_{0}\zeta-2\rho}{\rho^{2}}\partial_{y}\Psi.

Note that we used the conformality of
\rho^{2}g_{0}
with
g_{0}
and the fact that
\mathbb{S}^{2}
has dimension 2.

The regularity of
D[\zeta]\phi
can be deduced by a standard elliptic regularity argument using the expressions of
\Delta_{\mathrm{E}}
under the coordinate system
(x,y)
. The following proposition is proved similarly as Proposition 2.7. of
[
ABZ11
]
:

Proposition 6.1
.

Suppose
s>3
,
s^{\prime}\leq s
, and
\zeta\in H^{s+0.5}
,
\phi\in H^{s^{\prime}}
, such that
|\zeta|_{L^{\infty}}<1/2
. Then there is an increasing function
K
such that

\|D[\zeta]\phi\|_{H^{s^{\prime}-1}}\leq K\big(\|\zeta\|_{H^{s+0.5}}\big)\|\phi\|_{H^{s^{\prime}}}.

Furthermore, the linearization of
D[\zeta]\phi
can be computed similarly as in the Euclidean case. We directly state the following proposition, which resembles Proposition 2.11. of
[
ABZ11
]
; the proof is quite standard as in
[
Lan05
]
, and is omitted.

Proposition 6.2
.

Suppose
s>3
,
s^{\prime}\leq s
, and
\zeta\in H^{s+0.5}
,
\phi\in H^{s^{\prime}}
, such that
|\zeta|_{L^{\infty}}<1/2
. There is a neighbourhood
\mathfrak{U}
of
\zeta
in
H^{s+0.5}
in which the mapping

\mathfrak{U}\ni\zeta\to D[\zeta]\phi\in H^{s^{\prime}-1}

is differentiable, and the differential along direction
\zeta_{1}
is

-D[\zeta]\big(\mathfrak{b}\zeta_{1}\big)-\mathrm{div}_{g_{0}}(\mathfrak{v}\zeta_{1}),

where

\mathfrak{b}=\left(1+\frac{|\nabla_{0}\zeta|^{2}}{(1+\zeta)^{2}}\right)^{-1}\left(D[\zeta]\phi+\frac{\nabla_{0}\zeta\cdot\nabla_{0}\phi}{(1+\zeta)^{2}}\right),\quad\mathfrak{v}=\frac{\nabla_{0}\phi-\mathfrak{b}\nabla_{0}\zeta}{(1+\zeta)^{2}},

as in Theorem
1.1
.

In order to para-linearize the Dirichlet-Neumann operator, we will lift all the quantities of interest from
\mathbb{S}^{2}\times[-1/2,0]
to
\mathrm{SU}(2)\times[-1/2,0]
. Suppose
\Phi
is the velocity potential inside the region enclosed by
M_{\zeta}
, and
\phi(x):=\Phi\big((1+\zeta(x))N_{0}(x)\big)
is the Dirichlet boundary value of
\Phi
on
M_{\zeta}
. Then the corresponding pulled-back
\Psi
satisfies
\Delta_{\mathrm{E}}\Psi=0
on
\mathbb{S}^{2}\times[-1/2,0)
, and
\Psi(x,0)=\phi(x)
. Writing
\rho^{\sharp}=1+\zeta^{\sharp}+y
, defining the elliptic differential operator

(6.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}

\displaystyle:=\Delta_{G_{0}}+\left((\rho^{\sharp})^{2}+|\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}|_{G_{0}}^{2}\right)\partial_{y}^{2}-2\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}\cdot(\nabla_{G_{0}}\partial_{y})-\left(\Delta_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}-2\rho^{\sharp}\right)\partial_{y}

on
\mathrm{SU}(2)\times[-1/2,0]
, the lift
\Psi^{\sharp}
satisfies
\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}\Psi^{\sharp}=0
on
\mathrm{SU}(2)\times[-1/2,0)
, and
\Psi^{\sharp}(x,0)=\phi^{\sharp}(x)
. In fact,
\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}
is just the lift of
\rho^{2}\Delta_{\mathrm{E}}
. The quantity
D[\zeta]\phi
is lifted as the boundary value of

(6.6)

\mathcal{N}_{\zeta}\Psi^{\sharp}:=\left(1+\frac{|\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}|_{G_{0}}^{2}}{(\rho^{\sharp})^{2}}\right)\partial_{y}\Psi^{\sharp}-\frac{\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}\Psi^{\sharp}}{(\rho^{\sharp})^{2}}

at
y=0
. Our task now becomes the following:

Task 6.1
.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}
and
\mathcal{N}_{\zeta}
be as in (
6.5
) and (
6.6
). Find a suitable
\mathbf{T}_{3}
-invariant symbol
a
on
\mathrm{SU}(2)
such that if
\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}\Psi^{\sharp}=0
on
\mathrm{SU}(2)\times[-1/2,0)
, and
\Psi^{\sharp}(x,0)=\phi^{\sharp}(x)
, then

\mathcal{N}_{\zeta}\Psi^{\sharp}\big|_{y=0}=T_{a}\phi^{\sharp}+\mathbf{T}_{3}\text{-invariant smoothing terms}.

The rest of this section is devoted to finding out this paralinearization formula. The idea of accomplishing Task
6.1
is simple: considering the “height” coordinate
y
as a parameter of evolution, the quantity
\mathcal{N}_{\zeta}\Psi^{\sharp}
is the final state of an elliptic evolutionary problem
\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}\Psi^{\sharp}=0
; by suitable factorization of the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}
, the boundary value
\mathcal{N}_{\zeta}\Psi^{\sharp}
then admits an explicit expression.

The idea is exactly the one employed by, for example, Appendix C of Chapter 12 of Taylor’s book
[
Tay13
]
. Its para-differential version is exactly the argument employed by
[
AM09
]
and
[
ABZ11
]
. We will basically follow
[
AM09
]
and
[
ABZ11
]
, to whose idea our argument is very close.

From now on, we will fix a real number
s>3
. We assume that the height function
\zeta\in H^{s+0.5}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
, so that the surface
M_{\zeta}
is of class
C^{s-0.5}_{*}\subset C^{2.5+}
. We also assume that the boundary value
\phi\in H^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{2})\subset C^{s-1}_{*}(\mathbb{S}^{2})\subset C^{2+}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
. Although the lifting operator
\sharp
from functions on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
to functions on
\mathrm{SU}(2)
does not improve Sobolev regularity, it obviously does not undermine Zygmund regularity either:

Proposition 6.3
.

For
\mathbf{T}_{3}
-invariant functions on
\mathrm{SU}(2)
, the Sobolev embedding

H^{s}\big(\mathbf{T}_{3}\setminus\mathrm{SU}(2)\big)\subset C^{s-1}_{*}\big(\mathbf{T}_{3}\setminus\mathrm{SU}(2)\big)

is valid for
s>1
.

For example, we still have

\displaystyle\zeta^{\sharp}\in C^{s-0.5}_{*}\big(\mathbf{T}_{3}\setminus\mathrm{SU}(2)\big)\subset C^{2.5+}\big(\mathbf{T}_{3}\setminus\mathrm{SU}(2)\big),

\displaystyle\phi^{\sharp}\in C^{s-1}_{*}\big(\mathbf{T}_{3}\setminus\mathrm{SU}(2)\big)\subset C^{2+}\big(\mathbf{T}_{3}\setminus\mathrm{SU}(2)\big),

although
H^{s}(\mathrm{SU}(2))\not\subset C^{s-1}_{*}(\mathrm{SU}(2))
. From now on we we will always be dealing with symbols or functions on
\mathrm{SU}(2)
with
\mathbf{T}_{3}
-invariance described above, so this improved Sobolev embedding always holds.

6.2.
Alinhac’s Good Unknown

We turn to the para-linearization of equation
\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}\Psi^{\sharp}=0
. We shall look at the Laplace equation
\Delta_{\mathrm{E}}\Psi=0
inside
M_{\zeta}
, and after lifting to
\mathrm{SU}(2)\times[-1/2,0]
, we shall find the corresponding
good unknown
in the sense of Alinhac
[
Ali89
]
; that is, we need to find the optimal unknown function which eliminates all loss of regularity due to the non-smooth coordinate change. The idea was originally introduced by Alinhac in
[
Ali89
]
, Subsection 3.1.. Lannes
[
Lan05
]
noticed its importance to the study of the Dirichlet-Neumann operator. The para-differential version of the good unknown was employed by
[
AM09
]
and
[
ABZ11
]
. We will basically follow the approach of
[
AM09
]
and
[
ABZ11
]
.

We start by finding out a harmonic function inside
M_{\zeta}
that envelopes all information of the normal direction velocity. We observe a nice commutation property between
\Delta_{\mathrm{E}}
with
\rho\partial_{[}\rho]
, the infinitesimal generator of dilation:

\big[\rho^{2}\Delta_{\mathrm{E}},\rho\partial_{[}\rho]\big]=(2-2\rho^{2})\Delta_{\mathrm{E}},

we find that
\rho\partial_{\rho}\Phi
is a harmonic function inside
M_{\zeta}
. Since
\rho=1+\zeta(x)+y
for our choice of coordinates
(x,y)\in\mathbb{S}^{2}\times[-1/2,0]
, by pulling back to
\bar{\mathcal{W}}
, it follows that

\Delta_{\mathrm{E}}\mathfrak{B}=0,\quad\text{where}\quad\mathfrak{B}=\rho\partial_{y}\Psi.

Lifting to
\mathrm{SU}(2)\times[-1/2,0]
, setting
\rho^{\sharp}=1+\zeta^{\sharp}+y
, we find

\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}\mathfrak{B}^{\sharp}=0,\quad\text{where}\quad\mathfrak{B}^{\sharp}=\rho^{\sharp}\partial_{y}\Psi^{\sharp}.

We define scalar functions

(6.7)

\displaystyle\beta_{1}(y;x)

\displaystyle=\big(\rho^{\sharp}(x,y)\big)^{2}+|\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}(x)|_{G_{0}}^{2},

\displaystyle\beta_{3}(y;x)

\displaystyle=-\Delta_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}(x)+2\rho^{\sharp}(x,y),

and then define a classical differential symbol

(6.8)

\displaystyle\beta_{2}(x,l)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{3}X_{j}\zeta^{\sharp}(x)\cdot\sigma[X_{j}](l)

\displaystyle=\text{the symbol of }\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}},

where the left-invariant vector fields
X_{j}
are as in (
5.3
). The operator
\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}
is now re-written as

\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}=\Delta_{G_{0}}+\beta_{1}\partial_{y}^{2}-2\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}\partial_{y}+\beta_{3}\partial_{y}.

We are now at the place to introduce the good unknown in the sense of Alinhac:

Proposition 6.4
.

Write
\mathfrak{B}=\rho\partial_{y}\Psi
. Defining the good unknown
W^{\sharp}:=\Psi^{\sharp}-T_{1/\rho^{\sharp}}T_{\mathfrak{B}^{\sharp}}\zeta^{\sharp}
and the para-differential operator on
\mathrm{SU}(2)\times[-1/2,0]

\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}:=\Delta_{G_{0}}+T_{\beta_{1}}\partial_{y}^{2}-2T_{\beta_{2}}\partial_{y}+T_{\beta_{3}}\partial_{y}

corresponding to
\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}
, we have

\big\|\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}W^{\sharp}\big\|_{C^{0}_{y}H_{x}^{s+0.5}}\lesssim K\big(\|\zeta\|_{H^{s+0.5}}\big)\|\phi\|_{H^{s}}

on
\mathrm{SU}(2)\times[-1/2,0]
, where
K
is an increasing function, approximately linear when the argument is small.

Remark 6.1
.

Another reason that
W^{\sharp}
has better regularity than
\Psi^{\sharp}
is that, by Theorem
4.2
, we have
T_{1/\rho^{\sharp}}T_{\mathfrak{B}^{\sharp}}=T_{\partial_{y}\Psi^{\sharp}}\mod\mathbf{Op}\Sigma_{<1/2}^{-(s-2)}
, so

W^{\sharp}=\Psi^{\sharp}-T_{\partial_{y}\Psi^{\sharp}}\zeta^{\sharp}\mod C^{0}_{y}H_{x}^{2s-1.5},

and the difference resembles the remainder term in Bony’s para-linearization theorem, i.e. Theorem
3.11
, for the composition
\big[\Phi\big((1+\zeta+y)N_{0}\big)\big]^{\sharp}
.

Notation 6.1
.

For any index
s^{\prime}>0
, we use

\mod C^{0}_{y}H_{x}^{s^{\prime}}\quad\text{or simply}\quad\mod H_{x}^{s^{\prime}}

to refer to equality between quantities whose difference has
C^{0}_{y}H_{x}^{s^{\prime}}
(or
H^{s^{\prime}}_{x}
) norm on
\mathbf{T}_{3}\setminus\mathrm{SU}(2)
controlled by
K\big(\|\zeta\|_{H^{s+0.5}}\big)\|\phi\|_{H^{s}}
.

Proof of Proposition
6.4
.

Throughout the proof, we employ the “mod” notation defined in
6.1
.

The proof is divided into three steps.

Step 1: intermediate equation for
\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}w^{\sharp}
.
Since
\Delta_{\mathrm{E}}\Psi=0
, by exactly the same argument as in
[
ABZ11
]
, Section 3, we obtain

\nabla_{0}\Psi,\,\partial_{y}\Psi\in C^{0}_{y}H^{s-1}_{x}(\mathbb{S}^{2}),\quad\partial_{y}^{2}\Psi\in C^{0}_{y}H^{s-2}_{x}(\mathbb{S}^{2}).

Upon lifting to
\mathrm{SU}(2)
, it follows that

\nabla_{G_{0}}\Psi^{\sharp},\,\partial_{y}\Psi^{\sharp}\in C^{0}_{y}H^{s-1}_{x}\big(\mathbf{T}_{3}\setminus\mathrm{SU}(2)\big),\quad\partial_{y}^{2}\Psi^{\sharp}\in C^{0}_{y}H^{s-2}_{x}\big(\mathbf{T}_{3}\setminus\mathrm{SU}(2)\big).

In fact

\big\|\nabla_{G_{0}}\Psi^{\sharp}\big\|_{C^{0}_{y}H^{s-1}_{x}}+\big\|\partial_{y}\Psi^{\sharp}\big\|_{C^{0}_{y}H^{s-1}_{x}}\lesssim\|\phi\|_{H^{s}},\quad\big\|\partial_{y}^{2}\Psi^{\sharp}\big\|_{C^{0}_{y}H^{s-2}_{x}}\lesssim\|\phi\|_{H^{s}}.

Using the para-product estimate, i.e. Theorem
3.10
and Remark
3.5
, since
\zeta^{\sharp}\in H^{s+0.5}\big(\mathbf{T}_{3}\setminus\mathrm{SU}(2)\big)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}\Psi^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=\big(\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}-\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}\big)\Psi^{\sharp}\quad\text{(precise equality)}

\displaystyle=-\left(T_{\partial_{y}^{2}\Psi^{\sharp}}\beta_{1}-2\sum_{j=1}^{3}T_{X_{j}\partial_{y}\Psi^{\sharp}}X_{j}\zeta^{\sharp}+T_{\partial_{y}\Psi^{\sharp}}\beta_{3}\right)\mod C_{y}^{0}H_{x}^{s+0.5}.

Thus we compute

(6.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}w^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}\left(\Psi^{\sharp}-T_{1/\rho^{\sharp}}T_{\mathfrak{B}^{\sharp}}\zeta^{\sharp}\right)\quad\text{(precise equality)}

\displaystyle=-\left(T_{1/\rho^{\sharp}}\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}T_{\mathfrak{B}^{\sharp}}\zeta^{\sharp}+T_{\partial_{y}^{2}\Psi^{\sharp}}\beta_{1}-2\sum_{j=1}^{3}T_{X_{j}\partial_{y}\Psi^{\sharp}}X_{j}\zeta^{\sharp}+T_{\partial_{y}\Psi^{\sharp}}\beta_{3}\right)

\displaystyle-\left[\mathcal{P}_{\zeta},T_{1/\rho^{\sharp}}\right]T_{\mathfrak{B}^{\sharp}}\zeta^{\sharp}\mod C_{y}^{0}H_{x}^{s+0.5}.

Step 2: elimination of commutator.
To eliminate the commutator
\left[\mathcal{P}_{\zeta},T_{1/\rho^{\sharp}}\right]T_{\mathfrak{B}^{\sharp}}\zeta^{\sharp}
in (
6.9
), we need the composition formula for para-differential operators, i.e. Theorem
4.2
. The “classical version” of
[\mathcal{P}_{\zeta},T_{1/\rho^{\sharp}}]
is just
[\mathcal{L}_{\zeta},1/\rho^{\sharp}]
, whose projection to
\mathbb{S}^{2}\times[-1/2,0]
is simply

\left[\rho^{2}\Delta_{\mathrm{E}},\frac{1}{\rho}\right]=-2\partial_{y}\cdot\nabla_{\mathrm{E}}=-2\partial_{y}.

Here we used
\Delta_{\mathrm{E}}(1/\rho)=0
. Lifting to
\mathrm{SU}(2)\times[-1/2,0]
, it follows that
[\mathcal{L}_{\zeta},1/\rho^{\sharp}]=-2\partial_{y}
.

To pass to the para-differential version
[\mathcal{P}_{\zeta},T_{1/\rho^{\sharp}}]
, we shall keep exploiting the fact that
\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}
is a classical differential operator. Here with a little abuse of notation, given symbols
a,b
on
\mathrm{SU}(2)
leading to classical differential operators, we write
\{a,b\}
for
the symbol of the commutator

[\mathbf{Op}(a),\mathbf{Op}(b)]
(in usual notation the Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
only involves first order differentiation of symbols). Now writing down

(6.10)

\displaystyle\big[\mathcal{P}_{\zeta},T_{1/\rho^{\sharp}}\big]

\displaystyle=\big[\Delta_{G_{0}},T_{1/\rho^{\sharp}}\big]+\big[T_{\beta_{1}}\partial_{y}^{2},T_{1/\rho^{\sharp}}\big]-2\big[T_{\beta_{2}}\partial_{y},T_{1/\rho^{\sharp}}\big]+\big[T_{\beta_{3}}\partial_{y},T_{1/\rho^{\sharp}}\big],

it is not hard to see, with the aid of Theorem
4.2
, Formula (
5.7
) and also Remark
5.1
, that each term in the right-hand-side corresponds to exactly its classical counterpart in
[\mathcal{L}_{\zeta},1/\rho^{\sharp}]
with an affordable regularizing error.

For example, by assumption, the number
r:=s-1.5>1.5
, so with
\#_{r;\mathscr{Q}}
as in Formula (
5.7
), the third term in (
6.10
) becomes

\displaystyle\big[T_{\beta_{2}}\partial_{y},T_{1/\rho^{\sharp}}\big]

\displaystyle=T_{\beta_{2}}T_{\partial_{y}(1/\rho^{\sharp})}+T_{\beta_{2}}T_{1/\rho^{\sharp}}\partial_{y}-T_{1/\rho^{\sharp}}T_{\beta_{2}}\partial_{y}\quad\text{(precise equality)}

\displaystyle=T_{\beta_{2}\#_{r;\mathscr{Q}}\partial_{y}(1/\rho^{\sharp})}+T_{\{\beta_{2},1/\rho^{\sharp}\}}\partial_{y}\mod\mathbf{Op}\Sigma_{<1/2}^{-r}+\mathbf{Op}\Sigma_{<1/2}^{-r}\partial_{y},

while on the other hand

\displaystyle\left[\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}\partial_{y},\frac{1}{\rho^{\sharp}}\right]

\displaystyle=\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}\left(\partial_{y}\left(\frac{1}{\rho^{\sharp}}\right)\cdot\right)+\left[\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}},\frac{1}{\rho^{\sharp}}\right]\partial_{y}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Op}\left(\beta_{2}\#_{r;\mathscr{Q}}\partial_{y}(1/\rho^{\sharp})\right)+\mathbf{Op}\left(\{\beta_{2},1/\rho^{\sharp}\}\right)\partial_{y}

in
[\mathcal{L}_{\zeta},1/\rho^{\sharp}]
. Here we used the fact that
\beta_{2}
is the symbol of a classical first order differential operator. In other words, Remark
5.1
ensures that
[\mathcal{P}_{\zeta},T_{1/\rho^{\sharp}}]
must coincide with the para-differential operator corresponding to
[\mathcal{L}_{\zeta},1/\rho^{\sharp}]
, which is merely
-2\partial_{y}
, up to an error term:

\displaystyle\big[\mathcal{P}_{\zeta},T_{1/\rho^{\sharp}}\big]=-2\partial_{y}\mod\mathbf{Op}\Sigma_{<1/2}^{-(s-1.5)}\partial_{y}^{2}+\mathbf{Op}\Sigma_{<1/2}^{-(s-2.5)}\partial_{y}+\mathbf{Op}\Sigma_{<1/2}^{-(s-2.5)},

The reason that there is an error term of order
-(s-2.5)
is that the
\mathbf{T}_{3}
-invariant symbols are
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}
are of
H^{s-0.5}
regularity in
x
, while
\beta_{3}\in H^{s-1.5}\subset C_{*}^{s-2.5}
.

Since
\partial_{y}^{k}\Psi^{\sharp}\in C_{y}^{0}H^{s-k}_{x}\big(\mathbf{T}_{3}\setminus\mathrm{SU}(2)\big)
, we find that
\partial_{y}T_{\mathfrak{B}^{\sharp}}\zeta^{\sharp}=T_{\partial_{y}\mathfrak{B}^{\sharp}}\zeta^{\sharp}
is still of class
C_{y}^{0}H^{s+0.5}_{x}
. On the other hand, the function
\partial_{y}^{2}T_{\mathfrak{B}^{\sharp}}\zeta^{\sharp}=T_{\partial_{y}^{2}\mathfrak{B}^{\sharp}}\zeta^{\sharp}
is of class
C_{y}^{0}H^{s-0.5}_{x}
, since
\partial^{2}_{y}\mathfrak{B}^{\sharp}\in H_{x}^{s-3}\subset C^{-1}_{*}
, so the para-product operator
T_{\partial^{2}_{y}\mathfrak{B}^{\sharp}}
has order 1, according to Proposition
4.4
. Taking into account the regularizing operators, we successfully proved that the commutator
\left[\mathcal{P}_{\zeta},T_{1/\rho^{\sharp}}\right]T_{\mathfrak{B}^{\sharp}}\zeta^{\sharp}\in C_{y}^{0}H^{s+0.5}_{x}
. Summarizing, we find that (
6.9
) in fact implies

(6.11)

\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}w^{\sharp}=-\left(T_{1/\rho^{\sharp}}\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}T_{\mathfrak{B}^{\sharp}}\zeta^{\sharp}+T_{\partial_{y}^{2}\Psi^{\sharp}}\beta_{1}-2\sum_{j=1}^{3}T_{X_{j}\partial_{y}\Psi^{\sharp}}X_{j}\zeta^{\sharp}+T_{\partial_{y}\Psi^{\sharp}}\beta_{3}\right)\mod C_{y}^{0}H_{x}^{2s-2.5}.

Note that
s>3
, hence
2s-2.5>s+0.5
.

Step 3: final elimination.
Now similarly as in the proof of Lemma 3.17. in
[
ABZ11
]
, we compute

(6.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}T_{\mathfrak{B}^{\sharp}}\zeta^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=\left(\Delta_{G_{0}}+T_{\beta_{1}}\partial_{y}^{2}-2T_{\beta_{2}}\partial_{y}+T_{\beta_{3}}\partial_{y}\right)T_{\mathfrak{B}^{\sharp}}\zeta^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=2\sum_{j=1}^{3}T_{X_{j}\mathfrak{B}^{\sharp}}X_{j}\zeta^{\sharp}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}T_{X_{j}\zeta^{\sharp}\partial_{y}\mathfrak{B}^{\sharp}}X_{j}\zeta^{\sharp}+T_{\mathfrak{B}^{\sharp}}\Delta_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}\mod C_{y}^{0}H_{x}^{s+0.5}.

Here we used the
XT_{a}u=T_{Xa}u+T_{a}Xu
for a left-invariant vector field
X\in\mathfrak{su}(2)
, and the fact that
\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}\mathfrak{B}^{\sharp}=0
. Thus

(6.13)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}T_{\mathfrak{B}^{\sharp}}\zeta^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=2\sum_{j=1}^{3}T_{X_{j}\mathfrak{B}^{\sharp}}X_{j}\zeta^{\sharp}-T_{\partial_{y}\mathfrak{B}^{\sharp}}\beta_{1}-T_{\mathfrak{B}^{\sharp}}\beta_{3}

\displaystyle\mod C_{y}^{0}H_{x}^{s+0.5}

\displaystyle=2\sum_{j=1}^{3}T_{\rho^{\sharp}X_{j}\partial_{y}\Psi^{\sharp}}X_{j}\zeta^{\sharp}-T_{\rho^{\sharp}\partial_{y}^{2}\Psi^{\sharp}}\beta_{1}-T_{\rho^{\sharp}\partial_{y}\Psi^{\sharp}}\beta_{3}

\displaystyle\mod C_{y}^{0}H_{x}^{s+0.5}

Here in the first equality of (
6.13
), when computing

\displaystyle\beta_{1}

\displaystyle=|\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}|_{G_{0}}^{2}

\displaystyle\mod H^{s+0.5}_{x}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{3}|X_{j}\zeta^{\sharp}|^{2}

\displaystyle\mod H^{s+0.5}_{x}

\displaystyle=2\sum_{j=1}^{3}T_{X_{j}\zeta^{\sharp}}X_{j}\zeta^{\sharp}

\displaystyle\mod H^{s+0.5}_{x},

we used the para-linearization theorem of Bony, i.e. Theorem
3.11
; in the third equality, we used

\displaystyle 2\sum_{j=1}^{3}T_{\partial_{y}\Psi^{\sharp}X_{j}\rho^{\sharp}}X_{j}\zeta^{\sharp}-T_{\partial_{y}\Psi^{\sharp}}\beta_{1}

\displaystyle=2\sum_{j=1}^{3}T_{\partial_{y}\Psi^{\sharp}}T_{X_{j}\zeta^{\sharp}}X_{j}\zeta-T_{\partial_{y}\Psi^{\sharp}}\sum_{j=1}^{3}|X_{j}\zeta^{\sharp}|^{2}

\displaystyle\mod C_{y}^{0}H^{2s-2.5}_{x}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\mod C_{y}^{0}H^{2s-2.5}_{x}.

The proof is completed by noting that the right-hand-side of (
6.12
) cancels with (
6.11
) modulo
C_{y}^{0}H_{x}^{s+0.5}
, since
T_{1/\rho^{\sharp}}T_{\rho^{\sharp}}=\mathrm{Id}+\mathbf{Op}\Sigma_{<1/2}^{-(s-0.5)}
by Theorem
4.2
.
∎

6.3.
Factorization

With the elliptic para-differential equation satisfied by the good unknown
w^{\sharp}
at hand, we are now at the place to factorize the equation to obtain the behaviour of
\partial_{y}\Psi^{\sharp}
near the boundary
y=0
, to accomplish Task
6.1
. This is the essential step for which an explicit, global symbolic calculus on
\mathrm{SU}(2)
is necessary. The framework of our argument is parallel to Lemma 3.18. of
[
ABZ11
]
, but the details are quite different, since we are obviously working with a different geometry.

So we still assume
s>3
,
\zeta\in H^{s+0.5}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
, and set
\beta_{1}
,
\beta_{2}
and
\beta_{3}
as in (
6.7
)-(
6.8
). Imitating Lemma 3.18. of
[
ABZ11
]
, we seek for first-order rough symbols
a,A
on
\mathrm{SU}(2)
, depending on the parameter
y\in[-1/2,0]
, such that

(6.14)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{G_{0}}+T_{\beta_{1}}\partial_{y}^{2}-2T_{\beta_{2}}\partial_{y}+T_{\beta_{3}}\partial_{y}

\displaystyle=T_{\beta_{1}}(\partial_{y}-T_{a})(\partial_{y}-T_{A})+R_{0}+R_{1}\partial_{y}.

Here we write
\delta=\min(0.5,s-3)>0
, and suppose
R_{0}\in\mathbf{Op}\Sigma_{<1/2}^{0.5-\delta}
,
R_{1}\in\mathbf{Op}\Sigma_{<1/2}^{-0.5-\delta}
.

We need to apply Theorem
4.2
and the composition formula (
5.7
) on
\mathrm{SU}(2)
, with the left-invariant vector fields
\partial_{[}+,-,0]
given in (
5.4
) and RT-admissible tuple
\mathscr{Q}_{+,-,0}
given in (
5.6
). Suppose that
a,A
are both of class
C_{y}^{1}\mathcal{A}^{1}_{0.5+\delta}(\mathrm{SU}(2))
. Write
a=a_{1}+a_{0}
,
A=A_{1}+A_{0}
, with subscript
1,0
denoting symbols in
\mathcal{A}^{1}_{1.5+\delta}(\mathrm{SU}(2))
and
\mathcal{A}^{0}_{0.5+\delta}(\mathrm{SU}(2))
respectively. Then we propose the system to be solved as

(6.15)

\displaystyle a_{1}A_{1}+\sum_{\mu\in\{+,-,0\}}\mathbf{D}_{\mu}a_{1}\partial_{\mu}A_{1}+a_{1}A_{0}+a_{0}A_{1}-\partial_{y}A_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma[\Delta_{G_{0}}]}{\beta_{1}}

\displaystyle a_{1}+A_{1}+a_{0}+A_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\beta_{2}-\beta_{3}}{\beta_{1}}.

By Theorem
4.2
and Formula (
5.7
), we find that
T_{1/\beta_{1}}T_{\beta_{1}}=\mathrm{Id}\mod\Sigma_{<1/2}^{-(s-1.5)}
, so comparing symbols of same order, we find that the resulting
R_{0}
and
R_{1}
match with our requirement in (
6.14
).

Now we are at the place to solve (
6.15
). Preserving the highest order symbols, we obtain

\displaystyle a_{1}A_{1}=\frac{\sigma[\Delta_{G_{0}}]}{\beta_{1}},\quad a_{1}+A_{1}=\frac{2\beta_{2}}{\beta_{1}}.

Note that
\sigma[\Delta_{G_{0}}]=-l(l+1)\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}_{[}}l]
, so for each label of representation
l\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}_{0}
we solve, with the convention that the Hermitian part of
A_{1}(y;x,l)
is positive definite for
l
large,

(6.16)

\displaystyle a_{1}(y;x,l)

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta_{2}(x,l)}{\beta_{1}(y;x)}-\tilde{\lambda}_{1}(y;x,l)

\displaystyle A_{1}(y;x,l)

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta_{2}(x,l)}{\beta_{1}(y;x)}+\tilde{\lambda}_{1}(y;x,l).

Here we set

\tilde{\lambda}_{1}(y;x,l):=\frac{\sqrt{\beta_{2}(x,l)^{2}+\beta_{1}(y;x)l(l+1)}}{\beta_{1}(y;x)}:=\sqrt{\frac{l(l+1)}{\beta_{1}(y;x)}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\binom{k}{1/2}\left(\frac{\beta_{2}(x,l)^{2}}{\beta_{1}(y;x)l(l+1)}\right)^{k}.

At this level there is no issue of commutativity, as the matrix
\beta_{2}(x,l)
commutes with
\beta_{1}(y;x)l(l+1)
, which is simply a scaling transformation on each
\mathcal{H}_{[}l]
. Corollary
4.5.1
immediately implies that the power series in the definition of square root sums to a symbol of class
\mathcal{A}^{0}_{s-1.5}
. Thus the symbols
\tilde{\lambda}_{1}(y;x,l)
,
a_{1}(y;x,l)
and
A_{1}(y;x,l)
are indeed of class
\mathcal{A}^{1}_{s-1.5}
. This justifies our search for symbols
a,A
up to highest order.

Now the equations for
a_{0},A_{0}
read

\displaystyle a_{1}A_{0}+a_{0}A_{1}

\displaystyle=\partial_{y}A_{1}-\sum_{\mu\in\{+,-,0\}}\mathbf{D}_{\mu}a_{1}\partial_{\mu}A_{1}

\displaystyle a_{0}+A_{0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\beta_{3}}{\beta_{1}}.

The issue here is that the symbol
\partial_{\mu}A_{1}
does not commute with the symbol
\beta_{2}
anymore, though all other terms do. Fortunately, we may content ourselves with solutions up to error of order
-1
. Since
A_{1}
and
a_{0}
are of order 1 and 0 in the sense of Definition
2.6
, we find that
[a_{0},A_{1}]
should be a symbol of order
0
, thus negligible. Replacing
a_{0}A_{1}
by
A_{1}a_{0}+[a_{0},A_{1}]
, we can then re-write the system as

\displaystyle a_{1}A_{0}+A_{1}a_{0}

\displaystyle=\partial_{y}A_{1}-\sum_{\mu\in\{+,-,0\}}\mathbf{D}_{\mu}a_{1}\partial_{\mu}A_{1}\mod\mathcal{A}^{0}_{s-2.5}

\displaystyle a_{0}+A_{0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\beta_{3}}{\beta_{1}}.

Multiplying the second equation by
A_{1}
from the left, subtracting it from the first, we can explicitly solve
A_{0}
, and then
a_{0}
, up to an error in
\mathcal{A}^{-1}_{s-2.5}
.

It is thus safe to choose

(6.17)

\displaystyle a_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{1}^{-1}}{2}\partial_{y}A_{1}+\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{1}^{-1}\beta_{3}\beta_{2}-2\beta_{1}\beta_{3}+1}{2\beta_{1}^{2}}-\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{1}^{-1}}{2}\sum_{\mu\in\{+,-,0\}}\mathbf{D}_{\mu}a_{1}\partial_{\mu}A_{1}

\displaystyle A_{0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{1}^{-1}}{2}\partial_{y}A_{1}-\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{1}^{-1}\beta_{3}\beta_{2}+1}{2\beta_{1}^{2}}+\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{1}^{-1}}{2}\sum_{\mu\in\{+,-,0\}}\mathbf{D}_{\mu}a_{1}\partial_{\mu}A_{1}.

Applying Proposition
4.6
and the Corollary, we justify
a_{0},A_{0}\in\mathcal{A}^{0}_{s-2.5}
, and this final result justifies the legitimacy of neglecting
[a_{0},A_{1}]
.

To sum up, we have successfully obtained the desired factorization (
6.14
), with
a,A
given by (
6.16
)-(
6.17
), and

\|R_{0}\|_{H^{s+0.5-\delta}\to H^{s}},\,\|R_{1}\|_{H^{s-0.5-\delta}\to H^{s}}\lesssim K\big(\|\zeta\|_{H^{s+0.5}}\big)\|\phi\|_{H^{s}}.

The next step is to find out what the boundary value
\partial_{y}w^{\sharp}\big|_{y=0}
looks like. We state the following lemma, whose proof is identical to that of Proposition 3.19. in
[
ABZ11
]
:

Lemma 6.1

(Regularity for Evolutionary Elliptic Para-Differential Equation)
.

Suppose
r>1
, the
y
-dependent symbol
a\in C^{1}_{y}\mathcal{A}^{1}_{r}(\mathrm{SU}(2))
for
y\in[-1/2,0]
, such that the Hermitian part of
a
is
\geq c|l|
for some
c>0
when
l
is sufficiently large. Suppose
N<0
,
W\in C^{0}_{y}H^{-N}_{x}
solves the evolutionary elliptic para-differential equation

\partial_{y}W+T_{a}W=f\in C^{0}_{y}H^{s}_{x},\quad s\in\mathbb{R},

then in fact
W(0)\in H^{s+1-\varepsilon}
:

\|W(0)\|_{H^{s+1-\varepsilon}}\lesssim_{\varepsilon}\|f\|_{C^{0}_{y}H^{s}_{x}}+\|w\|_{C^{0}_{y}H^{-N}_{x}}.

With the factorization (
6.14
), we set
\tilde{W}=(\partial_{y}-T_{A})W^{\sharp}
and find

\partial_{y}\tilde{W}+T_{a}\tilde{W}=R_{0}\tilde{W}+R_{0}\partial_{y}\tilde{W}\mod C^{0}_{y}H^{2s-1.5}.

From the expression
a=a_{1}+a_{0}
, where
a_{1},a_{0}
are as in (
6.16
)-(
6.17
), we find that
a
in fact depends smoothly on
y
. On the other hand,
\tilde{W}\in C^{0}_{y}H^{s-1}_{x}
, with norm controlled in terms of
\|\zeta\|_{H^{s+0.5}}
and
\|\phi\|_{H^{s}}
, so we can apply Lemma
6.1
to conclude

(6.18)

\left\|\partial_{y}W^{\sharp}\big|_{y=0}-T_{A}W^{\sharp}\big|_{y=0}\right\|_{H^{s+0.5}}\lesssim K\big(\|\zeta\|_{H^{s+0.5}}\big)\|\phi\|_{H^{s}},

where

A=A_{1}+A_{0},

with
A_{1}
given in (
6.16
) and
A_{0}
given in (
6.17
). All the symbols and functions of concern are
\mathbf{T}_{3}
-invariant.

6.4.
Concluding the Proof of Theorem
1.1

We are at the stage to complete Task
6.1
. With a little abuse of notation, if
b=\sum_{j}b^{j}X_{j}
is a vector field, then we write

T_{b}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}=\sum_{j}T_{b^{j}}X_{j}.

Thus we compute, with Bony’s para-linearization theorem (and still the “mod” notation in
6.1
),

(6.19)

\displaystyle(\rho^{\sharp})^{2}\mathcal{N}_{\zeta}\Psi^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=\beta_{1}\partial_{y}\Psi^{\sharp}-\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}\Psi^{\sharp}\quad\text{(precise equality)}

\displaystyle=T_{\beta_{1}}\partial_{y}\Psi^{\sharp}+2T_{\partial_{y}\Psi^{\sharp}\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}-T_{\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}\Psi^{\sharp}-T_{\nabla_{G_{0}}\Psi^{\sharp}}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}\mod C_{y}^{0}H^{s+0.5}_{x}

Substituting in
\Psi^{\sharp}=W^{\sharp}+T_{1/\rho^{\sharp}}T_{\mathfrak{B}^{\sharp}}\zeta^{\sharp}
, we find that the second and third term in (
6.19
) cancel out the
\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}
within them:

T_{\partial_{y}\Psi^{\sharp}\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}-T_{\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}\Psi^{\sharp}=-T_{\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}W^{\sharp}\mod C_{y}^{0}H^{s+0.5}_{x}.

Thus using the fact
\mathfrak{B}^{\sharp},\partial_{y}\mathfrak{B}^{\sharp}\in C^{0}_{y}H^{s+0.5}_{x}
, (
6.19
) reduces to

(\rho^{\sharp})^{2}\mathcal{N}_{\zeta}\Psi^{\sharp}=T_{\beta_{1}}\partial_{y}W^{\sharp}-T_{\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}W^{\sharp}-T_{\nabla_{G_{0}}\Psi^{\sharp}-\partial_{y}\Psi^{\sharp}\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}\mod C_{y}^{0}H^{s+0.5}_{x}.

Using (
6.18
), substituting in (
6.16
)-(
6.17
) at
y=0
, we obtain the desired result: the Dirichlet-Neumann operator
{D[\zeta]\phi}
is lifted to
\mathrm{SU}(2)
as

(6.20)

\big(D[\zeta]\phi\big)^{\sharp}=T_{\lambda}\big(\phi^{\sharp}-T_{\mathfrak{b}^{\sharp}}\zeta^{\sharp}\big)-T_{\mathfrak{v}^{\sharp}}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}\mod H^{s+0.5}_{x}.

Here with
\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3}
given in (
6.7
)-(
6.8
),
\tilde{\lambda}_{1}
and
A_{0}
given in (
6.16
)-(
6.17
) (evaluated at
y=0
), we have

(6.21)

\displaystyle\lambda(x,l)

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta_{1}(x)\tilde{\lambda}_{1}(x,l)}{(1+\zeta^{\sharp})^{2}}+\frac{\beta_{1}(x)A_{0}(x,l)}{(1+\zeta^{\sharp})^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\beta_{2}(x,l)^{2}+\beta_{1}(x)l(l+1)}}{(1+\zeta^{\sharp})^{2}}+\frac{\beta_{1}(x)A_{0}(x,l)}{(1+\zeta^{\sharp})^{2}}

\displaystyle=:\lambda^{(1)}(x,l)+\lambda^{(0)}(x,l).

The quantities
\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{v}
on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
are

(6.22)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{b}

\displaystyle=\partial_{y}\Psi\big|_{y=0}=\left(1+\frac{|\nabla_{0}\zeta|^{2}}{(1+\zeta)^{2}}\right)^{-1}\left({D[\zeta]\phi}+\frac{\nabla_{0}\zeta\cdot\nabla_{0}\phi}{(1+\zeta)^{2}}\right),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{v}

\displaystyle=\frac{\nabla_{0}\Psi-\partial_{y}\Psi\nabla_{0}\zeta}{\rho^{2}}\Bigg|_{y=0}=\frac{\nabla_{0}\phi-\mathfrak{b}\nabla_{0}\zeta}{(1+\zeta)^{2}}.

Note that we used
T_{\beta_{2}}=T_{\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}
and
\beta_{1}(y;x)A_{1}(y;x,l)-\beta_{2}(x,l)=\tilde{\lambda}_{1}(y;x,l)
. When
\zeta
is close to 0, the error term is obviously quadratic in
(\zeta,\phi)\in H^{s+0.5}\times H^{s}
.

This finishes the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

7.
Para-differential Form of the System

In this section, we conclude the proof of Theorem
1.2
using the results we have obtained so far. Although the method is quite standard, the symmetrization process is more complicated than that in
[
ABZ11
]
due to non-commutativity of symbolic calculus on
\mathrm{SU}(2)
. We thus mainly focus on what is different from
[
ABZ11
]
due to this non-commutativity, and sketch everything else that is parallel.

7.1.
Para-linearizing the Full System

We start by deriving the para-linearized form of the full system (
EQ
). We still write
\rho=1+\zeta
,
\beta_{1}=\rho^{2}+|\nabla_{0}\zeta|_{g_{0}}^{2}
at the level set
\{y=0\}
. As a consequence of the para-linearization formula for the Dirichlet-Neumann operator, we already know that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\zeta^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=\big(D[\zeta]\phi\big)^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=T_{\lambda}\big(\phi^{\sharp}-T_{\mathfrak{b}^{\sharp}}\zeta^{\sharp}\big)-T_{\mathfrak{v}^{\sharp}}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}+f_{1}(\zeta,\phi),

where
\lambda,\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{v}
are as in (
6.21
)-(
6.22
),
f_{1}(\zeta,\phi)
is
\mathbf{T}_{3}
-invariant and

\|f_{1}(\zeta,\phi)\|_{H_{x}^{s+0.5}}\lesssim\left(\|\zeta\|_{H_{x}^{s+0.5}}+\|\phi\|_{H_{x}^{s}}\right)^{2}.

To proceed further, we introduce the actual
good unknown
of interest, being the boundary value of
W^{\sharp}
in Proposition
6.4
modulo
H_{x}^{s+0.5}
:

(7.1)

w^{\sharp}:=\phi^{\sharp}-T_{\mathfrak{b}^{\sharp}}\zeta^{\sharp}.

Then with
\lambda,\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{v}
as in (
6.21
)-(
6.22
), we find that the mapping

\left(\begin{matrix}\zeta^{\sharp}\\
\phi^{\sharp}\end{matrix}\right)\to\left(\begin{matrix}\zeta^{\sharp}\\
w^{\sharp}\end{matrix}\right)

is a diffeomorphism in a neighbourhood of
(0,0)\in H^{s+0.5}_{x}\times H^{s}_{x}
. In fact, the mapping
\phi^{\sharp}\to\phi^{\sharp}-T_{\mathfrak{b}^{\sharp}}\zeta^{\sharp}
is linear in
\phi^{\sharp}
, and estimates in Section
2
on the para-product operator imply

\|T_{\mathfrak{b}^{\sharp}}\zeta^{\sharp}\|_{H^{s}_{x}}\lesssim\|\zeta\|_{H_{x}^{s+0.5}}\|\phi\|_{H_{x}^{s}}.

Thus when
\zeta
is close to 0 in
H^{s+0.5}_{x}
, the linear operator
\phi^{\sharp}\to\phi^{\sharp}-T_{\mathfrak{b}^{\sharp}}\zeta^{\sharp}
is invertible, and the inverse is smooth in
\zeta^{\sharp}
. Consequently, the passage of unknown from
(\zeta^{\sharp},\phi^{\sharp})
to
(\zeta^{\sharp},w^{\sharp})
does not lose any information. It then suffices to derive an equation governing
(\zeta^{\sharp},w^{\sharp})
.

To derive the evolution equation for
w^{\sharp}
, we need to study the terms in
\partial_{t}\phi
separately. We start with the mean curvature.

Proposition 7.1
.

Suppose
s>3
,
\zeta\in H^{s+0.5}
. Still write
\rho=1+\zeta
, and
\beta_{1}
for the lift of
\rho^{2}+|\nabla_{0}\zeta|_{g_{0}}^{2}
. The mean curvature
H(\zeta)
admits a para-linearization formula

\big(H(\zeta)\big)^{\sharp}=-T_{h}\zeta^{\sharp}\mod H_{x}^{2s-3}.

Here
h=h^{(2)}+h^{(1)}
is a classical differential symbol, with
h^{(2)}=(\rho^{\sharp})^{3}\beta_{1}^{-3/2}(\lambda^{(1)})^{2}
being a multiplication of
(\lambda^{(1)})^{2}
, and
h^{(1)}
takes the form

h^{(1)}=\sum_{j=1}^{3}h_{1j}\big(\zeta^{\sharp},\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp},\nabla_{G_{0}}^{2}\zeta^{\sharp}\big)\sigma[X_{j}]+h_{10}\big(\zeta^{\sharp},\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp},\nabla_{G_{0}}^{2}\zeta^{\sharp}\big),

where the coefficients
h_{1j}
are all
C^{\infty}
in its arguments.

Proof.

We are going to use the first variation formula to compute the mean curvature. The area of
M_{\zeta}
is

A(\zeta)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\rho\sqrt{\rho^{2}+|\nabla_{0}\zeta|_{g_{0}}^{2}}d\mu_{0}.

Given any
\zeta_{1}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
, we consider the slightly varied surfaces
M_{\zeta+\theta\zeta_{1}}
, i.e. the surfaces given by the graphs
x\to(1+\zeta(x)+\theta\zeta_{1}(x))N_{0}(x)
. Recall that under the coordinate system
(x,y)
introduced in the previous section, the hypersurface
y=0
corresponds to the distorted sphere
M_{\zeta}
, and the vector field

\nabla_{\mathrm{E}}y=-\frac{\nabla_{0}\zeta}{\rho^{2}}+\left(1+\frac{|\nabla_{0}\zeta|_{g_{0}}^{2}}{\rho^{2}}\right)\partial_{y}

along
\{y=0\}
is perpendicular to the hypersurface. Thus

N(\iota)=\frac{\nabla_{\mathrm{E}}y}{|\nabla_{\mathrm{E}}y|}

is the unit outer normal vector field along
M_{\zeta}
. Note that

|\nabla_{\mathrm{E}}y|=\sqrt{1+\frac{|\nabla_{0}\zeta|_{g_{0}}^{2}}{\rho^{2}}}.

Under this choice of coordinate, the variational vector field of this variation is
\zeta_{1}\partial_{y}\big|_{y=0}
, so

N(\iota)\cdot\big(\zeta_{1}\partial_{y})=\frac{\zeta_{1}\rho}{\sqrt{\rho^{2}+|\nabla_{0}\zeta|_{g_{0}}^{2}}}.

The first variation formula of area then gives

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\theta}A(\zeta+\theta\zeta_{1})\Bigg|_{\theta=0}=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}H(\zeta)\zeta_{1}\rho^{2}d\mu_{0}.

On the other hand, a direct computation with integration by parts gives that the left-hand-side in the first variation formula reads

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\theta}A(\zeta+\theta\zeta_{1})\Bigg|_{\theta=0}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\left(-\rho\Delta_{g_{0}}\zeta+\frac{\rho\mathrm{Hess}_{g_{0}}(\zeta)(\nabla_{0}\zeta,\nabla_{0}\zeta)}{\rho^{2}+|\nabla_{0}\zeta|_{g_{0}}^{2}}+2\rho^{2}\right)\frac{\zeta_{1}}{\sqrt{\rho^{2}+|\nabla_{0}\zeta|_{g_{0}}^{2}}}d\mu_{0},

Thus the mean curvature operator reads

(7.2)

H(\zeta)=\frac{1}{\rho\sqrt{\rho^{2}+|\nabla_{0}\zeta|_{g_{0}}^{2}}}\left(\Delta_{g_{0}}\zeta-\frac{\mathrm{Hess}_{g_{0}}(\zeta)(\nabla_{0}\zeta,\nabla_{0}\zeta)}{\rho^{2}+|\nabla_{0}\zeta|_{g_{0}}^{2}}\right)+\frac{2}{\rho\sqrt{\rho^{2}+|\nabla_{0}\zeta|_{g_{0}}^{2}}}.

We lift (
7.2
) to
\mathrm{SU}(2)
. Recalling that
\sum_{j=1}^{3}X_{j}\zeta^{\sharp}\sigma[X_{j}]
, where the left-invariant vector fields
X_{i}
are as in (
5.3
), we compute the lift of the Hessian form as

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hess}_{G_{0}}(\zeta^{\sharp})(\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp},\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{3}\left(X_{i}X_{j}\zeta^{\sharp}-(\nabla_{X_{i}}X_{j})\zeta^{\sharp}\right)\cdot X_{i}\zeta^{\sharp}\cdot X_{j}\zeta^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Op}(\beta_{2}(x,l)^{2})\zeta^{\sharp}.

Here we used that
\nabla_{X_{i}}X_{j}=[X_{i},X_{j}]/2
(with respect to the bi-invariant metric) on the Lie group
\mathrm{SU}(2)
. Recalling the symbol
\lambda^{(1)}=\sqrt{\beta_{2}^{2}+\beta_{1}l(l+1)}/(\rho^{\sharp})^{2}
in (
6.21
), we find that the first term in (
7.2
) are lifted as

(7.3)

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}}{\rho^{\sharp}(x)\beta_{1}(x)^{1/2}}-\frac{\mathrm{Hess}_{G_{0}}(\zeta^{\sharp})(\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp},\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp})}{\rho^{\sharp}(x)\beta_{1}(x)^{3/2}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Op}\left(\frac{-l(l+1)}{\rho^{\sharp}(x)\beta_{1}(x)^{1/2}}-\frac{\beta_{2}^{2}(x,l)}{\rho^{\sharp}(x)\beta_{1}(x)^{3/2}}\right)\zeta^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{Op}\big(h^{(2)}(x,l)\big)\zeta^{\sharp}.

By Bony’s para-linearization theorem, (
7.3
) is then para-linearized as (with the “mod” notation in
6.1
used)

-T_{h^{(2)}}\zeta^{\sharp}+T_{\tilde{h}^{(1)}}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}+T_{\tilde{h}_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}\mod H^{2s-3}_{x},

where
\tilde{h}^{(1)},\tilde{h}_{0}
are
C^{\infty}
function of
\big(\zeta^{\sharp},\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp},\nabla_{G_{0}}^{2}\zeta^{\sharp}\big)
. Finally, the last term in (
7.2
) is also lifted and para-linearized similarly. This completes the proof.
∎

We are now at the place to para-linearize the second order terms in the expression of
\partial_{t}\phi
, and derive the evolution equation of
w^{\sharp}
.

Proposition 7.2
.

Suppose
s>3
and
\zeta\in H^{s+0.5}
,
\phi\in H^{s}
. The evolution of
w^{\sharp}
is given by

\partial_{t}w^{\sharp}=-T_{\mathfrak{v}^{\sharp}}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}w^{\sharp}-T_{h}\zeta^{\sharp}+f_{2}(\zeta,\phi).

The error term satisfies

\|f_{2}(\zeta,\phi)\|_{H^{s}_{x}}\leq K\big(\|\zeta\|_{H^{s+0.5}_{x}}\big)\|\phi\|_{H^{s}_{x}},

where
K
is an increasing function vanishing linearly at zero.

Proof.

We start by looking at the quadratic terms in the equation for
\partial_{t}\phi
. By a direct computation using our coordinate system
(x,y)
introduced in last section, we argue just as in Lemma 3.26 of
[
ABZ11
]
as follows. Still write
\rho=1+\zeta
. Then with

F(\mu,a,b,c)=\frac{\left(\mu^{2}a+b\cdot c\right)^{2}}{2\mu^{2}\big(\mu^{2}+|b|_{g_{0}}^{2}\big)},

the quadratic part in the right-hand-side of the equation for
\phi
in (
EQ
) may be written as

(7.4)

\displaystyle-\frac{|\nabla_{0}\phi|_{g_{0}}^{2}}{2\rho^{2}}+\frac{\left(\rho^{2}D[\zeta]\phi+\nabla_{0}\zeta\cdot\nabla_{0}\phi\right)^{2}}{2\rho^{2}\big(\rho^{2}+|\nabla_{0}\zeta|_{g_{0}}^{2}\big)}=-\frac{|\nabla_{0}\phi|_{g_{0}}^{2}}{2\rho^{2}}+F(\rho,D[\zeta]\phi,\nabla_{0}\zeta,\nabla_{0}\phi).

Bony’s para-linearization theorem implies that

(7.5)

\displaystyle\left(-\frac{|\nabla_{0}\phi|_{g_{0}}^{2}}{2\rho^{2}}\right)^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=-T_{(\rho^{\sharp})^{-2}\nabla_{G_{0}}\phi^{\sharp}}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}\phi^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=-T_{(\rho^{\sharp})^{-2}\mathfrak{v}^{\sharp}}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}\phi^{\sharp}-T_{(\rho^{\sharp})^{-2}\mathfrak{b}^{\sharp}\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}\phi^{\sharp}\mod H^{2s-3}_{x},

and (here subscript for
F
stands for partial differentiation)

\big(F(\rho,D[\zeta]\phi,\nabla_{0}\zeta,\nabla_{0}\phi)\big)^{\sharp}=T_{F_{\mu}^{\sharp}}\rho^{\sharp}+T_{F_{a}^{\sharp}}\big(D[\zeta]\phi\big)^{\sharp}+T_{F_{b}^{\sharp}}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}+T_{F_{c}^{\sharp}}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}\phi^{\sharp}\mod H^{2s-3}_{x}.

Since
\rho\in H^{s+0.5}_{x}
, we find
T_{F_{\mu}^{\sharp}}\rho^{\sharp}\in H^{s+0.5}_{x}
and thus can be disregarded. At
(\mu,a,b,c)=(\rho,D[\zeta]\phi,\nabla_{0}\zeta,\nabla_{0}\phi)
, we compute, recalling (
6.22
),

\displaystyle F_{a}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu^{2}a+b\cdot c}{\mu^{2}+|b|_{g_{0}}^{2}}=\mathfrak{b},

\displaystyle F_{b}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu^{2}a+b\cdot c}{\mu^{2}\big(\mu^{2}+|b|_{g_{0}}^{2}\big)}\left(c-\frac{(\mu^{2}a+b\cdot c)b}{\mu^{2}+|b|_{g_{0}}^{2}}\right)=\mathfrak{bv},

\displaystyle F_{c}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu^{2}a+b\cdot c}{\mu^{2}\big(\mu^{2}+|b|_{g_{0}}^{2}\big)}b=\frac{\mathfrak{b}\nabla_{0}\zeta}{\rho^{2}}.

Thus

(7.6)

\big(F(\rho,D[\zeta]\phi,\nabla_{0}\zeta,\nabla_{0}\phi)\big)^{\sharp}=T_{\mathfrak{b}^{\sharp}}\big(D[\zeta]\phi\big)^{\sharp}+T_{\mathfrak{b}^{\sharp}}T_{\mathfrak{v}^{\sharp}}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}+T_{(\rho^{\sharp})^{-2}\mathfrak{b}^{\sharp}\nabla_{G_{0}}\phi^{\sharp}}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}\phi^{\sharp}\mod H^{s}_{x}.

Combining (
7.5
) and (
7.6
), after suitable cancellation and commutation (causing acceptable error), we find that (
7.4
) lifts to
\mathrm{SU}(2)
as

-T_{\mathfrak{v}^{\sharp}}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}w^{\sharp}+T_{\mathfrak{b}^{\sharp}}\big(D[\zeta]\phi\big)^{\sharp}\mod H_{x}^{s}.

Consequently, noticing that
\partial_{t}w^{\sharp}=\partial_{t}\phi^{\sharp}-T_{\partial_{t}\mathfrak{b}^{\sharp}}\phi^{\sharp}-T_{\mathfrak{b}^{\sharp}}\partial_{t}\zeta^{\sharp}
, we find

\partial_{t}w^{\sharp}=-T_{\mathfrak{v}^{\sharp}}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}w^{\sharp}-T_{h}\zeta^{\sharp}+T_{\partial_{t}\mathfrak{b}^{\sharp}}\phi^{\sharp}\mod H_{x}^{s}.

The error caused by para-linearization is quadratic in
\zeta
and
\phi
, since the para-linearization already includes the linear part of the nonlinear expression. Finally, the regularity of
T_{\partial_{t}\mathfrak{b}^{\sharp}}\phi^{\sharp}
is obtained by applying Proposition
6.1
-
6.2
, identically as in Lemma 3.27. of
[
ABZ11
]
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Just as in
[
ABZ11
]
, we obtain the para-linearization of system (
EQ
), lifted to
\mathrm{SU}(2)
:

(7.7)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\zeta^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=T_{\lambda}w^{\sharp}-T_{\mathfrak{v}^{\sharp}}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}+f_{1}(\zeta,\phi)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}w^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=-T_{\mathfrak{v}^{\sharp}}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}w^{\sharp}-T_{h}\zeta^{\sharp}+f_{2}(\zeta,\phi),

where the errors
f_{1},f_{2}
takes the explicit form

(7.8)

\displaystyle f_{1}(\zeta,\phi)

\displaystyle=\big(D[\zeta]\phi\big)^{\sharp}-T_{\lambda}\big(w^{\sharp}-T_{\mathfrak{v}^{\sharp}}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}\zeta^{\sharp}\big)

\displaystyle f_{2}(\zeta,\phi)

\displaystyle=\left(-\frac{|\nabla_{0}\phi|_{g_{0}}^{2}}{2\rho^{2}}+\frac{\left(\rho^{2}D[\zeta]\phi+\nabla_{0}\zeta\cdot\nabla_{0}\phi\right)^{2}}{2\rho^{2}\big(\rho^{2}+|\nabla_{0}\zeta|_{g_{0}}^{2}\big)}+H(\zeta)\right)^{\sharp}+T_{\mathfrak{v}^{\sharp}}\cdot\nabla_{G_{0}}w^{\sharp}+T_{h}\zeta^{\sharp}-T_{\partial_{t}\mathfrak{b}^{\sharp}}w^{\sharp},

and are affordable and in fact quadratic:

\|f_{1}(\zeta,\phi)\|_{H_{x}^{s+0.5}}+\|f_{2}(\zeta,\phi)\|_{H_{x}^{s}}\lesssim\left(\|\zeta\|_{H_{x}^{s+0.5}}+\|\phi\|_{H_{x}^{s}}\right)^{2}.

We already know that (
7.7
) is equivalent to (
EQ
) at least when
\|\zeta(t)\|_{H^{s+0.5}_{x}}
remains sufficiently small. Thus we have successfully derived the para-linear form of (
EQ
).

7.2.
Adjoint of Para-differential Operators

In order to symmetrize the system (
7.7
), we need to understand the adjoint properties of the Dirichlet-Neumann operator and the mean curvature operator. We start from the former. We still employ the notation
\rho=1+\zeta
, and define the symbol
\lambda
as in (
6.21
). The following proposition reflects the self-adjointness of the Dirichlet-Neumann operator.

Proposition 7.3
.

Still fix
s>3
and
\zeta\in H^{s+0.5}
,
\phi\in H^{s}
. Then the symbol
\lambda
of the Dirichlet-Neumann operator satisfies

(7.9)

(\rho^{\sharp})^{-2}\lambda^{\bullet;1,\mathscr{Q}}=\lambda\#_{1;\mathscr{Q}}(\rho^{\sharp})^{-2}\mod\mathcal{A}^{-1}_{s-2.5}

Here the operations
\#_{r;\mathscr{Q}}
and
{\bullet;r,\mathscr{Q}}
are defined in (
5.10
)-(
5.11
). Thus the para-differential operator
T_{\lambda}
satisfies

T_{(\rho^{\sharp})^{-2}}T_{\lambda}^{*}=T_{\lambda}T_{(\rho^{\sharp})^{-2}}\mod\mathbf{Op}\Sigma^{-0.5}_{<1/2}.

Proof.

If we directly manipulate with (
6.17
) and (
6.21
), the algebra in the proof of (
7.9
) would be extremely involved. Thus we proceed differently by recalling the geometric interpretation of the Dirichlet-Neumann operator.

Assume for the moment that
\zeta
is
smooth
. Recall that the linear operator taking the Dirichlet boundary value
\phi
to the Neumann boundary value
\bar{\nabla}\Phi\cdot N(\iota)=\big(N_{0}\cdot N(\iota)\big)D[\zeta]\phi
on
M_{\zeta}
is self-adjoint with respect to the surface measure of
M_{\zeta}
; this is the starting point of our argument. Recall that
N_{0}\cdot N(\iota)=\rho/\sqrt{\rho^{2}+|\nabla_{0}\zeta|_{g_{0}}^{2}}
, while the surface measure of
M_{\zeta}
is
d\mu(\iota)=\rho\sqrt{\rho^{2}+|\nabla_{0}\zeta|_{g_{0}}^{2}}d\mu_{0}
. Consequently, we find, for smooth functions
\phi
,
\phi_{1}
defined on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
,
